# Spec, Nekko Agent

> **This is the source of truth for the project.** It describes *what* we're building and *why*, vision, users, journeys, the feature set, and what success looks like. It is **not** about stack or technical design (that's `TASKS.md`). It is a **living artifact**: every prompt that adds or changes a feature updates this file so it always describes the system as it actually is and intends to be. The verbatim origin ask lives in [original-prompt.md](../../obsurdian/projects/nekko-agent/original-prompt.md).

## Clarification answers remain visible in chat

Implemented, verification in progress: replies submitted through ask_user appear as visible user-facing Q/A transcript rows at the saved tool result's chronological position, including existing chats when reopened. Selected labels and freeform notes stay literal; skipped or unattended questions show an explicit unanswered state. Pending, failed, malformed and unrelated tool results do not invent a user response. Model-facing answer wrapper instructions remain hidden. This is a display-only projection of existing stored history, not a duplicate user message or a change to model context. Projected replies support ordinary text copying but cannot edit, rewind, split or reuse a nonexistent persisted user-message ID. Acceptance requires focused transcript tests and isolated matched before/after evidence. Parent inspection of the original contact sheet and narrow light capture confirmed visible chronological Q/A and honest skipped/unattended states. Current-main integration preserves compact delegation and composer containment; 957 desktop tests across 142 files, desktop typecheck, production app build and 52 real-renderer assertions passed after integration. Fresh matched integration captures were inspected in narrow light/dark; original wide/narrow contact sheet was inspected. Optimized transcript projection passes 958 desktop tests; fresh hosted integration gates remain pending. PR #512 remains draft and unmerged during this handoff; live-provider and cross-platform verification remain unverified.
## Chat completion X icons and idle auto-completion (2026-10-15)

`[implemented; focused tests, desktop suite, typecheck and build pass; matched isolated before/after captures of the header and card controls (wide light/dark) inspected; packaged app and native mobile not checked]` Users did not recognize the checkmark on chat windows/cards as a way to close their chats. The active-chat completion action in shared-renderer pane headers and chat cards/saved-chat rows now shows the existing X icon. This is an icon-only refactor: X still completes/archives the chat into Show completed with the existing 60-day retention and restoration; it does not permanently delete the chat or become board-only Minimize. Preserve accurate Complete labels/tooltips, keyboard shortcuts, configured Delete/trash/confirmation behavior, terminal Close, hover/focus behavior, save-failure recovery and completion exit timing. Unrelated success/completed-state checkmarks and native mobile clients are outside scope.

**Idle agents complete themselves.** Settings → General → **Complete idle agents after** offers 1 week, 2 weeks, 1 month (the default) and 3 months, or Never. An agent untouched for longer than that is completed exactly as its X would complete it: reversible, kept for 60 days, restorable from Show completed. Pinned chats, sub-agents (they go with their parent), task and training chats, and anything running or waiting for an answer are skipped; if the app cannot tell what is running, nothing is completed. The sweep runs at launch and every six hours while the app is open, closes those chats' windows, and reports the batch in one toast. It runs in the desktop/web renderer, so a host with no window open does not complete chats.

No new permissions, network calls, telemetry or dependencies; one optional setting, `autoCompleteAfterDays`. Improved discoverability is the intended outcome, not a validated usability claim. Tracked in [#533](https://github.com/nekko-labs/nekko-agent/issues/533) and [TASKS.md](TASKS.md#chat-completion-x-icons-533).

## Window lifecycle shortcuts and guidance (2026-10-12)

Implemented, verification in progress: the focused agent window supports Ctrl/Cmd+Shift+E to Complete, Ctrl/Cmd+Shift+M to Minimize, and Ctrl/Cmd+Shift+Delete to Delete. Hover titles and context menus name the action and its hotkey. Minimize removes the board window only, preserving the item in the Agent panel and any running terminal process. Complete saves a chat into its existing left-panel **Show completed** list. Terminals have Minimize and confirmed Close with the delete chord; terminal completion/history is unsupported because no terminal archive exists. Shortcuts ignore repeated/composing keys, hidden panes and open modal dialogs, and never operate on every window.

**Show tips** defaults on in Settings. The first successfully saved chat completion highlights its existing Show completed control in the left panel with a dismissible explanation. On narrow screens, it automatically reveals the existing vertical agent list drawer and scrolls the highlighted Show completed destination into view without taking focus or expanding completed chats. Closing the drawer keeps the tip pending and highlights the existing Open agent list (Agents) button; reopening restores the destination highlight. Dismiss or opening Show completed clears the current tip. Horizontal lists already expose the destination and do not need drawer guidance. The seen state persists locally and is not consumed by a failed completion save. The highlight is static and adds no animation. Permanent chat deletion and terminal Close use an in-app confirmation with Cancel focused first and an explicit **Skip future deletion confirmations** choice. That persisted choice is independent of Show tips and can be reset using **Confirm deletions** in Settings. No new network calls, permissions, terminal archives or external side effects are introduced by guidance. General cards omit the redundant No project attached text and its empty separator.

Harness security boundary: dynamic renderer arguments are JSON literals with HTML delimiters and Unicode line separators escaped before source construction. Labels are compared as data, never interpolated into selector syntax. Hostile labels, keys and nested reset data must round-trip without executing injected code. This applies to the disposable evidence harness, not a new product permission or execution capability; hidden non-focusable windows, isolated profiles and denied external network/permissions remain required.

Acceptance: verify focused-window routing with multiple chats, terminal minimize without process shutdown, cancel/confirm/skip/reset deletion, one-time completion tips after successful saving only, and matched isolated light/dark wide/narrow evidence. Native macOS/Linux keyboard behavior and Rust daemon terminal processes remain unverified locally.

Verification status: merged with main 76c2db7b; 974 desktop tests, desktop typecheck, production build and regenerated docs passed. Fresh isolated matched evidence has 8 baseline and 71 after assertions passing. A disposable Windows cmd PTY executed commands before and after renderer minimization using the TypeScript host backend. Fresh r5 drawer/settings/confirmation captures and sampled motion were inspected, published and verified. PR #510 landed as bdd75023; some non-required final-head checks were still running at merge and require follow-up. Native OS shortcut delivery and Rust daemon PTY survival remain unverified; terminal archive history is outside scope.

## Composer containment and compact subagent activity (2026-10-13)

Implemented and renderer-verified in PR #515; landing pending final documentation checks. The wall composer grows with new and wrapped draft rows, including queued prompts, attachments, skills and footer controls. A bottom-docked composer expands upward in normal layout and reserves space by reducing the board area, rather than covering agent windows. Deleting text shrinks it. Saved manual height remains a preference, not a hard ceiling that clips content. At a viewport-aware height cap only the draft editor scrolls: the composer shell and body never own another scrollbar, and text stays inside the composer ring. Top docking retains normal layout behavior.

Delegation activity is compact and collapsed by default while sending tasks or receiving large results. Its chat headline and subagent lists share a bounded plain-text, one-sentence activity summary and event-derived Working, Waiting, Completed, Failed or Stopped status. Questions/approvals override working status; terminal status is not inferred solely from a stored reply. Full task/result details remain available on explicit expansion with bounded scrolling. Summaries use existing local events and introduce no additional model calls or network disclosure. Ambiguous child associations must not display another child's status. This does not forcibly resize a user's entire agent pane.

Acceptance includes multiline/wrapping/long-token drafts, queued messages and attachments, manual sizing, shrink/cap and board displacement, plus synchronized child status transitions and large collapsed results. Matched isolated light/dark wide/narrow renderer evidence and typing motion are required. User screenshots are reference evidence, not implementation verification. Verified: 952 desktop tests, desktop typecheck/build, and 96 isolated renderer assertions with inspected matched light/dark wide/narrow captures and typing frames. Published media URLs were verified. Native macOS/Linux interaction and live-provider integration remain untested. Child identity is best-effort parent/title/creation timing; historical or ambiguous matches remain unknown until the transport provides an authoritative child ID.

## Automatic title requests during queued turns (2026-10-13)

Implemented, native macOS CI verification pending: automatic chat naming has one in-flight title request per session. A queued follow-up continues without waiting for that title request, but cannot start duplicate title work while it is pending. Failure, empty output or cancellation releases the claim so a later turn can try again. Eligibility is checked again after claiming; a user rename is never replaced by a pending automatic title result. Session-specific claims do not block naming in another chat. A held-response regression verifies queue progress before the title lands, plus failure/empty retry, cancellation and rename protection. This fixes the duplicate-request race exposed by macOS CI; it does not alter composer pixels or provider routing.

## Command wall state migration (data layer)

## Continue dependent work with GitHub PR stacks

Successful linking explicitly refreshes the existing PR list read in both the daemon and host fallback. The updated membership replaces the cached result, so the following ordinary poll keeps the GitHub stack label and merge control instead of resurrecting the old Link stack action. Background polling continues to use cached reads.

Implemented 2026-10-12. Ordinary new chats keep their existing worktree defaults. On an open PR's action panel, **Continue as stacked PR** starts a new chat in a separate worktree from that PR's committed head, retaining the selected model and tool/privacy preferences. It requires a selected local project with a matching GitHub remote and a PR whose head and base are in the same repository. An existing stack can be continued from its top PR. Closed/merged PRs, fork PRs, offline/incognito and sandbox sessions cannot start this flow. It never switches the user's branch or copies their uncommitted edits into the dependent branch.

The checkout records its parent PR, parent branch and commit, and configures the parent as the default base for PR creation. After the agent publishes the dependent PR, **Link stack** creates a native GitHub stack or appends it to its parent's existing stack. The parent must still be open, the new PR must belong to this chat's branch and target the parent, and an existing parent must be the top layer. Failures leave both PRs and local work intact and explain the recovery. Stack merges open GitHub's stack review screen because merging an upper layer can include every layer below it. Rebasing active worktrees after parent edits or remote merges stays explicit; no automatic reset, force push or deletion is performed. GitHub stack support is a public preview, requires GitHub authentication with PR write permission for publication, and is optional for users of other Git providers. Desktop and shared web UI are in scope; native mobile entry points are not part of this first version.

Both the local engine and host fallback provision pinned dependent checkouts. Restoring a removed checkout retains its parent instructions and branch. If its dependent branch was deleted, resuming fails with a recovery message rather than creating unrelated work from main; the user starts a new stacked chat explicitly.

Verified locally: matching parent code appears in the new checkout; source edits survive; normal worktree creation is unchanged; PR base is inherited; native linking supports retries and rejects unrelated branches, forks and wrong dependency order; stack merges cannot accidentally use the ordinary synchronous merge path. Focused tests cover real Git isolation and mocked native API creation, appending, authentication fallback and merge guards. Hidden full-renderer interaction checks and inspected screenshots cover 1200px/390px in light/dark, including error recovery, continuation, linking and privacy restrictions. A real continuation from PR #482 verified authentication, fetching, parent HEAD equality, a separate checkout, inherited default PR base and preservation of the source checkout in an isolated profile; its empty test checkout and profile were removed afterward. The native stack listing endpoint was also read successfully. Automated tests do not execute live GitHub stack mutations or merges. Native mobile entry points and automatic local restacking are deliberately outside this scope.

Implemented in the pure state/settings layer: persisted wall layout (`grid`, `focus`, or `fixed`, integer columns/rows clamped to 1–6), dock placement/visibility and six panel flags, independent panel minimization (`dock.minimized: Record<WallDockPanel, boolean>`), nullable hero session ID, and per-session companion-folded flags (`folded` never folds whole chats). Defaults are grid 3×2, right dock shown with every panel unminimized, all dock panels enabled except insights, no hero, and no folded companions. Minimization accepts only booleans for known panel keys; missing/invalid entries (including a global boolean) default to false without changing other panels. Fresh trees contain only chats and terminals. Legacy automations/insights leaves enable the corresponding dock flags and are removed while retaining surviving IDs, split directions, and relative sizes; legacy grid visibility is carried into those flags. Invalid persisted values fall back to safe defaults.

An optional finite non-negative `monthlyBudgetUsd` setting is an advisory USD budget, not a spending limit; invalid/unset values mean no budget. These preferences are local persisted data and introduce no network requests or new resource sampling. Acceptance is covered by pure migration/settings tests. The working implementation now includes Grid/Focus/Fixed controls, geometry transitions with reduced-motion support, a pinned existing composer without its tether, four-side dock controls, individual panel minimization, and spend/savings presentation using existing usage/resource APIs. Grid mode renders the Add cell at the same split-tree leaf where `addPane` will insert the next window, so the preview and actual placement match. The Hardware dock reads existing runtime status only: resident local-model count, reported resident memory, placement and last-used summaries come from `RuntimeStatus.resident`; last tokens/sec remains unavailable until a runtime status field reports throughput. Chat and composer contents are retained. Companions read and close through the existing workspace tree. Release acceptance remains unverified: actual-window before/after and motion evidence, independent horizontal Focus-deck scrolling, exact companion ownership in multi-chat workspaces, terminal deck output/model details, full performance regression checks, and whether local-runtime throughput should become a first-class reported field. Full terminal emulation and per-tool/per-pane provenance remain planned non-goals for this PR, not shipped claims.

## Page recovery and startup appearance (2026-10-12)

Settings and Nekko Server must show loading or a recoverable error instead of a blank page when the local service cannot answer. Settings uses the shared loaded preferences; a failed refresh preserves the last successful values, reports the problem, and offers Try again. Model-server polling preserves its last status during an outage and reports that it could not refresh. A rendering failure is contained to its page or section: navigation, neighbouring sections, and unrelated overlays remain usable. Retrying remounts the failed section. No fallback invents saved settings or a running server, changes credentials, downloads software, or sends diagnostic data remotely.

The initial loading/login UI resolves light or dark from the system preference before mounting. Fresh installs continue following system changes; an explicit theme remains authoritative. The shared desktop title bar uses the same pixel Nekko as Agents across every destination and retains the destination heading, version and native controls.

Implemented and checked with fresh Rust defaults, rejected/empty settings responses, rejected server status, retry recovery, a synthetic rendering failure, and matched hidden desktop/narrow captures in both themes. The friend's exact installed version, OS and original exception are unavailable; these checks verify the reproduced blank-page failure paths, not the cause of their service failure or a packaged-install upgrade. General async event-handler failures still need local handling; render boundaries do not intercept those.

## Vision

**Nekko Agent** is your local agent, backed by frontier intelligence. It is an open-source assistant for coding, cowork, and repeatable work that blends models running on your hardware with frontier providers. Use its own desktop UI or bring its capabilities into Claude Code, Cursor, Codex, OpenClaw, and other compatible tools through the CLI and MCP. Local-only work can run offline once its models and dependencies are installed; frontier calls and network integrations still require connectivity and may send their selected context to the configured provider.

The primary domain is **nekkoagent.com**; every other domain we own permanently redirects to it with paths and query strings preserved. The repository is **nekko-labs/nekko-agent**, the npm package and CLI command are **nekko-agent**, the bundle id is **dev.nekkolabs.nekkoagent**, and installers are named **NekkoAgent-…**.

The rename is finished and the legacy-name compatibility layer has been retired: only the `nekko-agent` command ships, only `NEKKO_*` environment variables are read, local data lives under `~/.nekko-agent` (desktop profile under `desktop/`, cloud deployments under `cloud/`, unless explicitly overridden), and only `nekko-agent://` deep links resolve. Existing Nekko Agent data from `~/.nekko` or its desktop app-data folder moves only after confirmation; profiles are never merged silently. Release assets and updater feeds published under an older name are immutable and stay where they are, and the earlier domains keep redirecting to the current one.

The personality is a capable, approachable assistant: clear task status, quiet confidence, and generous whitespace take precedence over spy or space metaphors.

Quality bar: on par with Zed / Cursor / Warp / OpenClaw, generous whitespace, minimal chrome, dark/light themes, fast.

The mascot redesign is in draft review: an icon-inspired 8-bit cat head in cream on dark green replaces the secret-agent accessories across desktop, live chat status, marketing, and mobile. It blinks, occasionally shows happy ^ ^ eyes, and uses stepped hops with reduced-motion support. The existing app icon remains unchanged. Cross-platform visual evidence and animation review are still required before shipping.

## Why It Exists

### Development build workflow

`npm run dev` runs `npm install` (about 2 seconds when nothing changed, so a pull that changed dependencies never launches against stale modules), then rebuilds the Rust daemon and every workspace the app imports before starting the desktop development server with hot reload, so it never runs stale output; a failed build prevents startup. It skips the production desktop bundle (electron-vite dev builds its own) and compiles the daemon with the `dev-daemon` Cargo profile (release optimizations without LTO, incremental), which the development app runs straight from `target/` as the newest local build: a one-file daemon change rebuilds in about 9 seconds instead of a minute. `npm run build` and packaging still use the release profile. Production renderer builds retain lazy diagram loading and all supported Mermaid layouts; build optimizations must preserve diagram rendering and normal application tree shaking.

People who want to run AI models locally are stuck choosing between two kinds of tool, each blind in its own way:

- **Local chat UIs (LM Studio, Ollama front-ends)** are *chat-only*, a great model runner with a chat box, but with no awareness of your files or projects. The model can talk about your work but can't actually do it.
- **Terminal CLIs (Claude Code, aider)** have agentic power but are *blind*, you can't see what changed without `git diff`, and editing means leaving the tool.

Nekko Agent exists to collapse that gap: easy local-model setup **and** a model that works inside your real codebases, with everything it does visible.

### Positioning (carry into website + README)

**AI help on your computer. For coding and everyday work.** Lead with what people can do: get help writing and coding, automate repetitive tasks, and choose AI running on their computer or an online service. Explain that a model is the AI they choose, and local AI runs on their own computer. Do not require readers to understand "frontier", "harness", "runtime", "CLI", or "MCP" before they understand the benefit. Technical setup details remain available farther down the page with short explanations.

The five main marketing headings are **Use AI on your computer or online**, **Choose the right AI for the job**, **Automate repetitive work**, **Manage your AI models in one place**, and **Control your computer by voice** (visibly Planned). Section titles describe concrete actions rather than agent/spy metaphors. Nekko Agent complements existing tools instead of requiring people to abandon their preferred interface; its differentiator remains combining available local compute with online models. Simplifying the language must preserve setup requirements, paid-provider costs, privacy boundaries, and the distinction between available and planned features.

### Messaging pillars

1. **Frontier and local, working together.** Use Nekko Agent as your primary harness/UI, or connect through its CLI and MCP from compatible tools such as Claude Code, Cursor, Codex, and OpenClaw. Bring your own frontier providers and local model servers.
2. **The right model for the task.** Help users select models for a prompt and delegate concrete work to local or cheaper models while reserving frontier capability for demanding planning. Local inference can avoid per-token API charges but still uses hardware, power, memory, and time. Explain routing decisions and never silently cross an offline boundary.
3. **Automation in one place.** Manage scheduled tasks, watchers, and workflows alongside interactive agent work. Reusable procedures support agent, skill, shell, and nested workflow steps with bounded retries and visible outcomes.
4. **Simpler model management.** Discover and connect local runtimes, manage supported model lifecycle actions from the same app, and mix managed and existing servers as support grows. Installing and supervising an inference runtime so a separate server app is unnecessary is a product goal, not a claim that an inference engine is already bundled.
5. **Control your PC offline, with safeguards.** Tell Nekko what you want by text or voice and let it perform supported local computer actions within permissions you choose. One-click guided setup selects a compatible local intent LLM, text-to-speech (TTS), optional speech-to-text (STT), and required runtimes based on the machine and each component's minimum requirements. The experience includes model/setup explanations, action previews, sensitive-action confirmations, and an immediate stop control. This is a major planned product capability, not available in the current release.

### Roadmap: beyond one machine

These are aspirations, not generally available capabilities or launch promises:

- **Team hardware and distributed work:** opt-in workers pool available machines for model inference, agent tasks, and workflows, with permissions, capacity reporting, isolation, cancellation, and clear ownership.
- **Local task runners:** expand today's local shell/agent workflow foundation into a practical alternative for jobs otherwise consuming hosted CI credits. GitHub Actions workflow compatibility and untrusted pull-request execution require their own design and security review; neither is implied by local shell steps.
- **Team workflows:** shared definitions, run coordination, access controls, and auditable outcomes across a team.
- **Optional cloud runners:** explore only if users want them. A paid service must remain optional and must not weaken the local-first experience.
- **Phone app as its own client:** `[in build 2026-10-03]` the phone app no longer wraps the desktop renderer. It is a purpose-built native app (see "Your agent in your pocket" below) that drives the user's own machine over the relay, in the spirit of Claude Code on the phone: start, follow, approve, and steer runs, with push when a run finishes. Philip chose (2026-10-03) to put **on-device local models** in the same app rather than a separate product, so the phone keeps working when the computer is off. Still roadmap: the same client for **cloud runners**, and MLX on iPhone if llama.cpp's Metal path falls short.
- **Native desktop shell:** a GPU-rendered native UI (GPUI or similar) remains an option once the Rust engine daemon owns the engine. It is deferred: the desktop UI stays web technology (Electron + React) for maintainability, and the speed contract below is met there first.

Existing cloud prototypes and older branding elsewhere in this spec describe implementation history or parallel work, not a requirement to launch a hosted product before the local Nekko Agent experience.

## Who It's For

- **Developers who want to use local models for real work**: people running LM Studio / Ollama / vLLM who are tired of a chat box that can't touch their files, and want an agent that reads, edits, and runs in their codebases.
- **Claude Code / aider / terminal-CLI users** who want the same agentic power but with an IDE-like surface (file tree, diffs, inline edit, visible context).
- **Privacy-conscious / offline users** who want everything to stay on their machine, and, with Cloud, a zero-data-retention path that still keeps inference local.
- **People who want an approachable local PC assistant** without assembling an intent LLM, speech models, runtimes, and hardware settings themselves. Voice is optional; typed control and visible confirmations must remain first-class.
- **People who want to reach their home/office model from their phone** (the headline Cloud feature).

## User Journeys & Experiences

### Editions, same engine + UI, different runtime

Nekko Agent ships the **same engine + same React UI** in several runtimes; from the user's point of view it's the same product wherever it runs (only the host transport differs, see `TASKS.md` and [spec-web-and-hosted.md](spec-web-and-hosted.md)):

- **Desktop** (Electron), the primary experience, available today (installers on GitHub Releases).
- **Self-hosted web**: `npx nekko-agent` or `npm run web` starts a local server that serves the same UI in a browser; offline-first.
- **Docker**: `docker compose up`, workspaces as mounted volumes, local models reachable via `host.docker.internal`.
- **Nekko Agent Cloud** (paid), managed hosting with subscriptions, an always-available **Zero-Data-Retention** mode, cloud chat-history + file management, and **phone connectivity to your locally-running model** via a secure relay.

### Window shortcuts and cloud pricing comparisons

Implemented; actual App renderer KeyboardEvent-to-store acceptance verified in a hidden isolated fixture for Ctrl/Cmd chords, aliases, default prevention, Alt exclusion and onboarding suppression. Physical OS delivery and visual acceptance remain unverified: Ctrl/Cmd+T creates a new agent and Ctrl/Cmd+Shift+T creates a new terminal through existing wall creation actions. Earlier N, backtick and J aliases remain. Setup onboarding suppresses global actions.

The Budget dock uses a money icon and a dated, bundled OpenRouter catalog snapshot for exact published model token rates, including versioned subscription API equivalents and identifiable local open models. No pricing network request occurs during normal use. Subscription usage remains included in the plan ($0 incremental API spend); monthly fees are not inferred or included. Unknown local models use the explicitly selected fallback only; comparison groups include Fable/Astra, Opus/Sol, Sonnet, Haiku/Luna and open models. These are user-selectable comparison groupings, not verified capability rankings or automatic guesses from model size. Users can disable the fallback. Exact source ids and legacy family estimates are distinguished. Standard text-token estimates exclude per-request long-context premiums, tools, batch discounts, hardware and electricity, and are not invoices. Ollama cloud pricing is linked as an alternative source, not silently substituted for OpenRouter rates. Unpublished sizes, fine-tunes and provider aliases without an unambiguous match remain unpriced or fall back for local usage only.

### Background notification and panel batch (2026-10-13)

Implemented, unit and fixture interaction tested; not landed. **Utilization** displays a Claude subscription credential once when enabled cloud provider entries share the same nonempty `tokenKey`, preferring the `anthropic` entry. Different token keys/accounts and API-key entries remain distinct; this is display-only and does not delete or change provider configuration.

**Budget** defaults to 1m and uses inclusive UTC daily buckets for today, 1wk (7 days), 1m (30), 6m (180), 1y (365) and all-time through today. Every recorded value follows the selected range: spend, input/output tokens, top agent by API cost, local tokens, local/subscription API-equivalent savings, fallback/unpriced-token counts and the set of models shown in the pricing comparison. Published unit rates and the fallback selection are configuration/reference values, not ranged spending. The active range uses an accent tint and pressed state. Missing daily detail from older hosts is unavailable rather than replaced with lifetime totals. TS host and Rust usage summary now emit matching per-day `bySessionCost`, `localTokens`, `avoidedCosts` and `modelIds`, with golden parity coverage. This supersedes earlier mixed ranged/lifetime Budget details.

Desktop notification suppression now combines a shell-only preload report (chat view and active session) from an owned main frame with Electron's actual visible, unminimized, focused window state. Wall selection conservatively suppresses nothing because selected panes may be collapsed or offscreen. Reports are cleared on reload, renderer loss and window destruction. The main-owned engine socket remains independent of renderer lifetime, and authoritative settings are read for each notice. Focused unit tests cover suppression and reporter trust. Isolated hidden/nonfocusable Electron integration verifies real TS backend agent-event websocket delivery, engine survival with no renderer, settings opt-out/re-enable, and injected native adapter show/click/disposal. It triggers actual provider-admission errors, not model inference, and does not emit OS toasts. Fresh hidden daemon-mode injected integration also passes the same 14 checks with no owned process survivors and removal of isolated home/data/profile. Actual OS focus and toast delivery/click evidence remains unverified. The separate `notification-native.yml` workflow is the sole native acceptance path, restricted to explicitly opted-in GitHub-hosted Windows with independent launcher, Electron child and PowerShell gates. It requires visible unique OS UI Automation text, a real OS click received by Electron, and an OS foreground handle matching the isolated fixture; a show event or screenshot alone cannot pass. Unsupported/noninteractive desktops fail with blocker evidence rather than a skipped pass. The injected launcher preserves `--daemon` and `--built-backend` but no longer supports `--native-ci`. The latest hosted setup failed before toast delivery: UIA Invoke returned without exposing a DND control and taskbar hit-target verification refused the mouse fallback. Setup now uses the semantic Win+N shell shortcut after an ineffective Invoke, only behind the same strict disposable hosted CI gates, and uses it to close only an observed panel. Shortcut return alone is not success: an explicit DND control and verified Off state remain mandatory. Mocked orchestration is tested; native acceptance of this fallback, installed-app identity/deep-link activation and OS sleep survival remain unverified.

PR #514 merge followup (2026-10-13): current-main integration preserves notification, picker, utilization and ranged Budget scope. Native acceptance remains blocked because hosted UIA Invoke and Win+N did not expose a DND control; no local OS settings or user-app changes are authorized by this followup. A usable disposable interactive Windows desktop with verified DND-Off, unique toast text, actual click and matching foreground handle is required. Current-head streaming frame-work p95 is 10.7 ms against the unchanged 10 ms gate; latency-only diagnostics do not attribute it. Failure diagnostics now profile streaming on both base and head with required release nekkod; no gate is weakened and no regression fix is claimed.

PR #514 safe followup after #512 (2026-10-13): merged main `ffd999d4` preserving the notification/panel feature and #512 question/answer transcript scope, both walkthroughs and performance histories. Windows run 38382178477 failed the first timezone parity test after 5.873s in an unbounded synchronous child, while subsequent cases took 83-101ms. The test now collects Date results asynchronously in bounded setup children grouped by timezone (10s child limit, 35s setup limit), retaining all seven exact zone/cutoff assertions and their default assertion deadlines. Local 11-test host safety suite passes; hosted cold-start confirmation remains pending. This is a harness fix, not a product cutoff change. Artifact analysis records full streaming p95 10.48ms (attempts 10.48/10.48/10.6), diagnostic base/head 13.09/12.02ms, and shared rendering/DevTools costs; no causal runtime fix or pass is established. Performance reports now distinguish measured checkout from workflow SHA without weakening budgets. Native run 38382178460 still failed before any toast because no explicit DND control was exposed after UIA Invoke/Win+N. No local OS send/settings mutation, blind native rerun, new visual acceptance, ready action or merge is authorized. Required native proof and complete visual inspection/publication remain blockers.

PR #514 reconciliation with #510 (2026-10-13): merged main `bdd75023` while retaining the complete notification, anchored picker, utilization and ranged Budget scope alongside lifecycle shortcuts, completion guidance and independent deletion confirmations. The desktop test command retains both notification safety and lifecycle harness checks, and the walkthrough documents both batches. After rebuilding shared, root typecheck, 131 focused desktop tests, 8 host usage tests and 24 safety/docs checks pass; regenerated docs are current. This behavior-preserving reconciliation adds no new visual design and is not fresh native or visual acceptance evidence. Current-head hosted gates must be checked after push; native delivery/click/focus proof and full affected-surface evidence remain release blockers. No user app interaction, OS send/settings mutation or native workflow replay is authorized by this reconciliation.

Separate local manual acceptance exception: `node apps/desktop/scripts/notification-native-local.cjs --allow-local-notification-test` requires explicit Windows opt-in and sends one silent real toast through the production native adapter using fresh isolated home/data/profile paths. Only a real manual toast click opens `Nekko notification test (isolated)` for a 20-second parent capture hold. The click wait is 90 seconds, with a 120-second hard deadline and scratch cleanup. It does not run CI OS helpers, automate OS input, change DND/settings, start the normal app/provider/network, or replay automatically. This is a local diagnostic path, not a replacement for the gated CI native acceptance path described above. Show/failed/click logs and post-click renderer capture are distinct from native-window evidence. The authorized local run recorded Electron show but no click within 90 seconds; it exited not verified, removed the profile, and captured no renderer/native window. Installed identity, actual visible delivery, focus and click acceptance remain unverified.

Matching before/after captures and recording are available but not inspected. Native OS toast testing is pending. Parent owns evidence acceptance, review resolution and landing; this batch does not imply OS sleep survival.

### Inline agent-window creation

The 2026-10-06 inline Add-tile placement is superseded by the 2026-10-13 anchored dropdown; its earlier verification applies only to that prior design. Implemented, unit and fixture interaction tested: the toolbar ellipsis opens a viewport-bounded dropdown anchored to its button and portalled outside wall clipping. Chat, Media and Terminal categories remain horizontal (scrollable at narrow widths). Opening it does not move or resize wall tiles, alter the saved split tree, or switch Focus to Grid. Outside pointer and Escape dismiss it. Keyboard opening focuses a choice; Escape/Close dismisses and restores the opener. Pending creation rejects double activation and disables picker dismissal; failures stay visible for retry. Chat selection uses the shared Auto model sentinel, never a literal model named `auto`. Failed chat configuration attempts to delete the newly created record before retry. If cleanup also fails, the mounted wall retains that unconfigured record and reuses it on retry rather than creating a duplicate; the error explicitly reports cleanup failure. This retry reference is local to the mounted view, not durable across navigation/restart. Wall entries and session lists deduplicate by id.

Chat offers an inline model catalog and existing non-archived chats; Media creates a dedicated image session; Terminal creates or opens a shell. The workspace plus menu offers exactly Agent, Media gen (a dedicated image session), and Terminal. Shell types are chosen under Settings > Terminal > Default shell, with Auto-detect as the default. Opening the menu does not launch a shell or change settings. The composer no longer switches an existing conversation between image and chat types; image parameters remain available within image sessions. No model inference, download or shell starts merely by opening the picker, and selection does not edit provider defaults or favorites.

Mounted synthetic Electron verification now covers actual CommandCenterView creation/options failure and retry, cleanup-failure duplicate prevention, toolbar/Escape dismissal and focus restoration, selected-model chat, image and terminal creation, existing-chat deduplication, Grid/Focus transitions, and workspace image entry through the real store. The fixture denies external requests and uses a fresh isolated profile; no real shell/provider calls occur. For the prior inline design, matched current-main/after full-view light/dark wide/narrow captures and sampled motion frames were directly inspected for clipping, contrast, placement and transitions. For the 2026-10-13 dropdown batch, 48 hidden isolated fixture interactions pass at 1200 and 400 CSS pixels in light/dark, covering bounds, unchanged wall geometry, dismissal/focus return, Budget ranges and Notifications settings. Matching before/after captures and a recording exist, but image and motion inspection remain pending; this batch is not visually accepted or landed. Reduced-motion suppression and narrow reachability pass mounted checks. Verification uses a non-activating off-screen Windows Electron window; native mobile UI is unchanged. Synthetic host behavior is not proof of real provider/shell integration. Video is **planned, unavailable and disabled**; no video generation or shipped status is claimed.

### Chat suggestions, progress and code access

Updated 2026-10-02. Suggested follow-up replies sit inside the top of the message box, above attachments and the draft. They keep their one-click send behavior and the Right Arrow draft suggestion. Image chats do not show reply suggestions.

Nekko gives a concise progress message before starting tool work and between meaningful action batches. Live updates remain visible in their original order between tool groups; tools and private reasoning stay in their own disclosures. Active status labels describe current work (Reading, Searching, Editing, Thinking, Writing reply), without appending "done" to an ongoing activity.

File tools work within the chat's project by default. Searches can target a single file or directory, missing paths produce an explicit error, and large source files can be read by inclusive line ranges instead of becoming inaccessible after a character cutoff. Shell guidance names the platform's actual shell. ChatGPT conversations preserve assistant commentary/final-answer phases so a progress-only response continues the task rather than prematurely ending it.

### Server, model browser and desktop tray

Requested 2026-10-01. The Model server and Agent server cards share a row above the model library on wide screens and stack on narrow screens. The model server uses the same power icon as existing runtime controls. Server settings save automatically after two seconds without edits or when a field loses focus; invalid values and failed saves remain visible without discarding the draft.

The model library is a two-pane browser: searchable model-family rows on the left, selected model details on the right. Downloaded families and individual variants show checkmarks. Quantizations, conversion publishers, formats and local folder providers for the same identifiable model share a family row, with their separate load, download and setup options under Variants. Different parameter sizes and trained models remain distinguishable. GPU guidance says "Fits on GPU", "Fits on GPU, but limited context size", or "Too big" when evidence supports it; CPU-only and unknown hardware are named honestly, and catalog estimates are not presented as measured context limits.

The sidebar plus menu closes after the pointer leaves the button or list, with a 220 ms grace period to cross the gap; re-entering cancels dismissal. The top-level sidebar workspace plus creates a new workspace with a terminal rather than inserting one into the active workspace. Repository-row controls are distinct: their plus creates an agent chat, and their terminal icon creates a shell, both in the clicked repository. The workspace list also shows saved chats whose windows are closed, grouped under their project, so reopening an older conversation does not require remembering its title in another view. Completed chats stay out of this list. Completing a session uses an X control (see *Chat completion X icons*) and moves it to the Completed list, where it stays read-only and restorable for 60 days before deletion. Completing a chat from its card, its saved-chat row or the row menu plays an exit on the row: it folds shut toward its left edge, a little stardust drifts out of where it vanished, then the space closes and the rows below slide up. The chat is saved as completed only after the row has gone; if saving fails the row comes back. The exit takes about 600 ms, the stardust fades within 900 ms, and both are skipped when reduced motion is preferred. The stored archivedAt field remains compatible with existing sessions. Chat headers put folder and branch details directly after the title, followed by a subtle slash divider before actions.

Desktop keeps a Nekko Agent tray icon. Its first action opens a new chat, followed by opening the UI, stopping/starting or restarting the Nekko service, and starting/stopping the model server. Closing the desktop window hides it to the tray while the service and running work remain alive. Explicit Quit shuts down the service and managed processes; the tray's Stop Nekko service stops the service without quitting the UI. Stopping the service is distinct from hiding the window, and restarting restores the UI connection rather than leaving a stale endpoint. Close-to-tray is existing behavior, not newly implemented by the 2026-10-13 notification batch. Keeping the UI hidden does not promise continued execution during OS sleep, shutdown or logout.

**Close guidance and playful farewell (2026-10-15).** `[implemented, focused tests and isolated Electron lifecycle checks; native desktop capture and real Windows notification click verification pending]` The first user close with a running service highlights Nekko's system-tray presence with a Windows balloon and a local companion explaining that agents remain running. The companion stays until acknowledged and offers **Got it**, **Shut down on close instead** (saves the preference and quits immediately), and **Settings**. **Settings > Desktop behavior** selects keep-running-in-tray (default) or shut-down-on-close; explicit Quit and external installer/migration close requests still quit. **Settings > Fun** groups the existing mascot toggle with a separate default-on **Tray farewell** toggle. Subsequent hides show the smiling, blinking pixel Nekko with a varied goodbye for two seconds, without taking focus; reduced-motion disables blinking. Disabling farewell suppresses that decoration, not the first-close explanation. The companion anchors to the reported tray bounds, clamped to that monitor's work area, falling back to the work-area corner when no tray bounds are available; Windows controls overflow-tray visibility, so exact placement above a hidden icon is not guaranteed. The explanation is deferred while the service is stopped. Notification clicks explicitly restore/focus the main UI, never an auxiliary browser/preview/companion window, and open/focus the corresponding existing agent workspace pane (or open that chat if its pane was closed).

### Running a model with nothing else installed

**Nekko Server (2026-09-30).** The former Model Server destination becomes Nekko Server. On wide screens its left column combines Model server and Agent server controls, each independently switchable, with runtime installation, endpoint settings, CLI and MCP access. The right column holds the searchable model library, downloads and folders. Narrow screens stack these sections while preserving every control. Navigation destinations can be dragged into a user-chosen order, with keyboard reordering and persistence across restarts; hidden experimental destinations keep their saved positions.

**Local image generation.** Image models use the optional stable-diffusion.cpp runtime rather than llama.cpp. Before installing it, the app shows its release version, hardware backend and full download size including runtime-library companions. Downloading an image model recommends the runtime but does not silently install it or prevent downloading weights alone. A managed runtime can be uninstalled without deleting models, and external binaries are never removed. Image models can be loaded and used through the model server's OpenAI-compatible image-generation endpoint, or from chat. `[shipped 2026-10-01]` Every chat has a **MultiModal | Image** switch in the composer. An Image chat sends each prompt to a local image model instead of a chat model: the composer's controls become the image model (image models only), size, steps, CFG and seed, with a one-press download when the model lacks its text encoders, and the agent-only controls (tool mode, tools, MCP, reasoning, effort, attachments, Automate) step aside. Each reply is the picture, shown with the model, size, steps, CFG, the seed actually used and the time it took, so any image can be reproduced; prompts and pictures stay together as the chat's history. Settings are kept per chat and picking a model adopts its family's tuned steps and CFG. The Command Center shows an Image chat as its own kind of worker (🎨), with the image model and count, thumbnails in place of text turns, a live "Loading the image model" / "Generating" line while it works, and a reply box that generates the next image. Nekko Server keeps each image model's setup (companions, paths, memory) and opens an Image chat on it with **Generate in chat**. Decision models are not a chat type: they answer typed questions about a state rather than converse, so they belong to the agent's tools and a test panel, not the composer. Missing text encoders or VAE files are named with configurable companion paths; borrowed model folders remain read-only. `[shipped 2026-10-01]` For the families it knows (FLUX.2 klein 4B and 9B, SD3.5, FLUX.1), the Generate panel lists the text encoders and VAE the model needs, which are already on disk, and what the rest weighs, and one press downloads them into a Nekko-owned folder shared across the family; the panel also starts from the family's tuned steps and CFG. A VAE whose Hugging Face repo is gated is fetched with the user's token when there is one, and otherwise a small openly licensed decoder (TAESD) stands in so the model still runs, which the panel says.

**Runtime updates.** An external GitHub Actions job checks upstream releases on a schedule and also accepts dispatch events. A newer usable release creates or updates a PR to the runtime-version manifest, not an automatic download or an in-app polling loop. Checks run every six hours and keep a seven-day release-age buffer before recommending new binaries; already newer pins are never downgraded. A GitHub App installed only on this repository creates the PRs with Contents and Pull requests write permission, triggering normal CI without enabling the broader GITHUB_TOKEN PR-creation/approval option. Credential provisioning is a separate confirmation-gated activation step. Incomplete releases are skipped; the PR goes through verification before becoming the recommended install.

**One user-data home.** Desktop, web and CLI default to `~/.nekko-agent`, including settings, sessions, tokens, runtime installs, launcher metadata and desktop profile data. Managed models live under `models/{type}` (chat, vision, embedding, image, audio, draft or unknown). Explicit data-directory overrides and borrowed model folders remain supported. Existing Nekko Agent desktop data and `~/.nekko` can be moved only after user confirmation, with conflict refusal, recoverable migration and saved references preserved. No old-brand compatibility is reintroduced.

**Consolidating earlier installs.** `[implemented, headless-verified; desktop dialog and a real terminal run unverified]` The single-profile move above refuses a destination that already has data, which leaves anyone who ran several earlier names (`~/.nekko`, `~/.agent-nekko`, `%APPDATA%\@agent-nekko`, `@kotrain`, `@open-paw`, `@nekko`, an installed Kotrain or Open Paw, their updater caches and `kotrain-*` temp folders) with data split across folders. Two entry points do the same additive merge. **Desktop:** every launch, before the engine starts, checks that those locations exist (existence checks only) and, if any hold data, asks: *Merge and clean up*, *Merge, keep old copies*, or *Not now*. *Not now* is not remembered, so the question returns next launch; nothing is hidden or silenced. **Terminal:** `nekko-agent migrate [--dry-run] [--yes] [--keep-old] [--no-restart]` lists what it found, and if the desktop app is running closes it (a normal close first, forced only after 20 seconds), merges, cleans up and reopens it. It reopens a packaged app; a development build is not relaunched, and the command says so. The normal close is a `taskkill` (no `/F`) to the app's main process only; the app treats a bare close request from another program as *quit*, not *hide to tray*, so the engine shuts down cleanly in well under a second (measured 0.4 s with a page open in Browser, against 20.5 s and a forced kill before). A person closing the window (close button, Alt+F4, taskbar Close window, which arrive as SC_CLOSE or no WM_CLOSE) still hides it to the tray. `[implemented, unit-tested, measured on Windows against an isolated desktop app]` The launch prompt is never shown when the data folder is set explicitly (`NEKKO_DATA_DIR`): the old folders belong to the real profile and must not be merged into, or deleted for, a scratch one.

The merge never overwrites: sessions and files already in `~/.nekko-agent` win; usage and quota logs are unioned line by line (spending history is the union of every source); providers, workspaces, favourite models, prompts, connectors and MCP servers are unioned by id, name or path while scalar settings keep the current value and gain only keys the current file lacks; sign-ins are merged by key, the newer wins; managed models move into `models/{type}`; an old folder's engine build moves over only when `~/.nekko-agent` has none, and otherwise is a superseded download that is neither mixed into the current build nor a reason to keep the old folder; saved paths that pointed into an old folder are rewritten; a workspace pointing at an earlier repo name follows the rename when the new folder exists. Per-machine state (`window-state.json`, `cli-link.json`, `migration.json`) is not merged. Before anything changes, the files that will change are copied to `~/.nekko-agent/backups/legacy-<time>/` (sessions, settings, tokens, usage; model files are moved, not copied). Cleanup deletes a folder only after confirming everything in it is present in `~/.nekko-agent`, runs an install's own silent uninstaller first, and keeps (and reports) anything it cannot prove is merged. It refuses to start while another Nekko process is serving from any involved folder. A failed merge deletes nothing and re-running continues.

After the merge, duplicates are folded (`profile-repair.ts`): folders by path (case, separator and trailing-slash insensitive on Windows; drive roots kept), local servers by kind and address (`localhost` = `127.0.0.1`, `/v1` ignored), subscriptions only when both tokens are known to belong to the same account (never on a guess). The id with the most history survives; sessions, usage, quota history and favourites are rewritten to it so totals are unchanged. `scripts/repair-profile.mjs` applies the same fold to an already-merged profile (dry run by default, `--keep`, `--remove`, `--account`, refuses while the app is serving). `[implemented, unit-tested]`

**Folder list hygiene.** `[implemented, unit-tested]` The host's `saveSettings` keeps one entry per folder on every write, and the composer's folder picker shows a folder saved twice once. Revoking a folder with its × keeps the menu open.

**Model picker resilience.** `[implemented, unit-tested; live check against an unreachable LAN server done via the app API]` Each provider's model list fills in as it answers instead of waiting for all; core list requests time out after 5 s (ChatGPT catalog 8 s, falling back to the curated list). An unreachable server no longer leaves the whole picker, including the default model, on "loading…".

**Agent window companions.** `[implemented; renderer-verified in the web edition, Electron webview unverified]` Changes (with its count), Files and Browser sit in each agent window's header beside Logs; the Agents title bar no longer carries them. On the wall the companion column has a 26 px close button and a drag/keyboard resizer (20–75 %, per chat, double-click resets). Files opens the chat's own checkout of its primary folder, else the primary folder, else its first supporting folder, and follows a folder change. A no-op workspace update no longer re-renders every wall window.

**Dev launcher banner.** `[implemented, verified in a terminal]` `npm run dev` prints an ASCII banner once the app reports in: version, pid, API URL (or that it is off), renderer URL, data folder, and the stop keys (q / Enter clean stop, Ctrl+C twice to force). No colour when `NO_COLOR` is set or output is not a terminal.

Out of scope and unverified: macOS and Linux paths are covered by unit tests with injected directories but not run on those systems; `Nekko Paw` and `Nekko Notes` installs are not touched (separate products); the Windows uninstaller call is not exercised in tests.

The **Nekko Server** tab contains the **Nekko Agent engine**: the model server Nekko Agent runs itself, so the answer to "how do I run a local model" is no longer "first install LM Studio". It is llama.cpp underneath, and everything around it (getting the engine, getting models, keeping them, serving them) happens in the app. It sits beside **Model Providers**, which is the other question entirely — where models come *from* (a Claude subscription, an API key, a server on another machine) — and the two used to be one crowded page.

**Getting an engine.** The card names the build this machine should run in terms of the hardware rather than the acronym ("An NVIDIA GPU with a current driver", not "CUDA"), picked from what the GPU probe actually found: CUDA only where an NVIDIA reading exists, Vulkan for a GPU we cannot identify, Metal on Apple Silicon, CPU when there is no evidence of anything else, and the rest of the list still offered for anyone who wants to override. A few hundred megabytes come from llama.cpp's own releases, and only after the user presses the button. Anyone who already has a `llama-server` can point at it instead, and a binary they point at is theirs: we run it and never update or remove it.

**Getting models.** Models are **one surface, not two tabs**: a single search box filters the models already on this machine and searches Hugging Face at once, so "do I have it" and "can I get it" are the same question, answered in one scroll with the installed models on top and the catalog below. An empty box opens the catalog on a short curated list, because the question a new user has is "what should I run", not "what exists". The sizes are explained in place rather than assumed known: the parameter count is labelled as billions of parameters (a bigger model knows more and needs more memory), each model's smallest build is checked against this machine's actual memory pool and chips as **fits here** / **tight fit** / **needs more memory**, and the model page marks the build most people should take (Q4_K_M when the repo publishes it, otherwise the lightest build that fits) with one-line notes on what the other quantizations trade. A row answers "which of these" and nothing more; opening one gives the model **its own page**, because "should I run this" needs what a row has no space for: the publisher's model card, how many people pull it and how many like it, when the repo last moved, what it was converted from, its license, and the full ladder of builds with the real download size and per-build fit verdict of each. The card is rendered from markdown we parse ourselves rather than injected as markup, images in it are dropped rather than fetched, and any HTML the publisher wrote is reduced to its text — it is prose from a stranger's repository, and it is treated that way. Downloads resume after an interruption, report their rate and time left, cancel for real, and land only after the file parses as a GGUF, so a half-finished model never appears in the library wearing the right name. `[updated 2026-10-01]` A finished download is also the moment the engine knows the file's geometry, so it plans the fullest fit the GPU can hold (every layer offloaded when they fit) and saves that as the model's preset, and **Start with Nekko** switches itself on, so the model a user just asked for runs next time the app opens with nobody visiting settings first. A machine whose hardware probe cannot answer skips the preset rather than writing a guess.

**Models you already have.** A GGUF is a GGUF whoever downloaded it, so a machine that has already pulled Qwen through Ollama or LM Studio should not have to pull it again to run it here. On first run Nekko Agent looks in the usual places for **Ollama, LM Studio, Jan, GPT4All, llama.cpp, KoboldCpp, LocalAI, Bionic, text-generation-webui and the Hugging Face cache vLLM serves from**, and every one of those folders that actually has models in it becomes a row in the library, named, sized and loadable. Ollama gets its own reader: it stores models as content-addressed blobs with no extension, so a directory walk finds a pile of hashes, and the manifests are read instead so `llama3.2:latest` appears under the name you would type at `ollama run`.

**What each file actually is.** A GGUF is not always a chat model: people also keep image generators, speech recognizers, embedding models and speculative-decoding draft heads on disk, and llama.cpp cannot serve most of those. The library reads each file's declared architecture, names what it is on the row (**chat**, **vision**, **embedding**, **image gen**, **audio**, **draft head**), and when the engine cannot serve it says why in a sentence ("an image-generation model, it runs in stable-diffusion.cpp") instead of offering a Load that can only fail. Vision models that need an `mmproj` projector say whether it is present and offer to fetch it from the model's own repository; companion files fetched that way land in a Nekko-owned folder, never in the borrowed app's. Chat templates and projectors found beside the model or in its tokenizer config are picked up at load and passed as `--mmproj` and `--chat-template-file`, so a borrowed vision model answers with working eyes rather than llama.cpp's opaque missing-file failure.

The **Folders** tab is that list, and all of it is editable: retype a path, switch a folder off without forgetting it, delete a row, or add one nobody guessed. Each row shows what it contributes (how many models, how many gigabytes) and whether it is on disk at all. The folder downloads land in is pinned at the top and cannot be deleted, because a download has to have somewhere to go. Everything else is **read, never written**: a borrowed model loads here exactly like one we downloaded, says which app it came from, and offers no delete, because that file belongs to that app and removing it from here would break it silently. A GGUF anywhere else can still be adopted by path without being copied.

**Two control surfaces over one state.** **Simple** is a single question, *how much of this machine may it use?*, and one slider from "leave my machine usable" to "everything it has". It solves through the same fit planner into a real context length, GPU offload and KV cache type, then says what it chose and what it costs in plain words ("32k of context, entirely on the GPU. About 7.3 GB of VRAM, leaving 12.1 GB for everything else."). It deliberately does not predict tokens per second: memory can be measured and speed would be invented. **Advanced** is every flag llama.cpp takes, context length, GPU offload, KV cache type, parallel slots, flash attention, batch and physical batch, CPU threads, mmap and mlock, RoPE base and scale, seed, and idle TTL. They are the same state, not two modes: moving the slider fills the fields, editing a field moves the projection, and settings can be saved as a model's default so it loads the same way every time. The same drawer answers the other half of "how should this model behave when I am not looking": **load when the engine starts** and **keep it loaded** sit together with the missing-projector fetch, because startup and eviction are one question asked about the same model. `[updated 2026-10-02]` The Advanced surface leads with a **Runs on** pick, GPU only, Hybrid or CPU only, instead of a bare layer number: GPU only writes an explicit all-layers offload rather than trusting the engine's default to agree, Hybrid reveals the layer split and the CPU threads it needs, and on a machine the planner found no GPU on, the two GPU choices disable rather than offer a load that could never use one.

**Serving.** The engine presents **one address** (`http://127.0.0.1:11500/v1` by default) that any OpenAI-compatible tool can point at, with a `llama-server` process per loaded model behind it. The address and port are shown whether the server is running or stopped, so another tool can be configured before the engine ever starts; the default port is a shared constant, never re-typed per surface. Several models stay resident at once; a request for one that is not loaded loads it; an idle model is evicted on a timer; and the address stays valid across loads and unloads, so a chat or an external tool configured once keeps working. Models marked **on start** load in order with their saved settings when the engine starts, a failure logged and never blocking the rest; and the library's **In memory** section shows what is resident (when it loaded, when it was last used, how much VRAM and context it holds, when it expires) with an unload action and a per-model idle timeout that can be changed without a reload. `[updated 2026-10-02]` A model still on its way in appears there the moment the load starts rather than at the next refresh: a progress bar with a percentage where the drop in free VRAM can be measured against the share of the file headed to the GPU, an indeterminate bar where it cannot (a CPU load, or no GPU probe), and a queue note while it waits behind another load. Each resident row says how long it has been idle and when it will evict, in words a person can't misread. Flags are written in the dialect the installed build speaks: newer llama.cpp takes `--load-mode` where the builds before it took `--no-mmap` and `--mlock`, and the engine probes `--help` once per binary so either era launches cleanly. A failed load is kept on the model's row as a readable reason, translated from llama.cpp's log where a known cause matches, rather than lost. The left column's **Model server settings** disclosure carries what every other local model server puts in its own app: port, whether it is reachable from the network or only this computer, an API key, allowed browser origins, how many models may be resident, the idle timeout, load-on-demand, start-with-the-app, and where models are kept. The defaults are the safe ones, and the two settings that can expose the machine say so in the sentence next to them rather than in a tooltip. `[updated 2026-10-15]` **Reaching the engine from the network requires a key** (#549): the router only checks a Bearer token when a key is set, so a LAN bind with an empty key would have put every route on the network for anyone to call. The engine (daemon side, in both the Rust and TS hosts) now refuses to start or reconfigure with **This computer and my network** selected and no key, returning a sentence the settings card shows rather than silently listening open; the last good listener stays up when a reconfigure is refused. The settings card does not leave the user to run into that refusal: picking **This computer and my network** with an empty key field fills in a generated 32-character key, a key already typed is kept, and switching back to **This computer only** never touches the key. `[updated 2026-10-01]` The card's own header answers the two questions asked first: a sliding switch turns the server on and off with its state (**serving** / **stopped**) named immediately beside it, and **Start with Nekko** sits on the same row rather than inside the settings disclosure, because whether it will be on tomorrow is as important as whether it is on now.

Because the file is ours, its GGUF header is read directly, so the layer count, KV head count, head dimension and trained context are **facts rather than gaps**: the memory projection for a model the engine holds is exact where the same projection for another server's model has to report parts of itself as unknown. After a load the real residency is measured against what was projected, and the difference feeds the same calibration record the other runtimes use.

**The fastest local tokens/s** `[in progress]`. With one user, every engine runs into the same wall (memory bandwidth), so Nekko Agent competes with Ollama, LM Studio and vLLM on what an agent actually does rather than on kernels it did not write. Measured results, method and the places it is not ahead are in [docs/benchmarks.md](docs/benchmarks.md).

- **The fastest backend for this machine, picked for you.** llama.cpp with CUDA on NVIDIA, and Vulkan on AMD and Intel GPUs, found from the operating system's own adapter list so those machines are no longer offered the CPU build. `[shipped 2026-09-30]` On an Apple Silicon Mac, **MLX** is offered as an optional runtime beside llama.cpp: one press installs it with its own Python (the system's is never touched), MLX model folders already on the Mac (LM Studio's MLX downloads included) appear in the library tagged MLX, and they run through the same engine address as every other model. `[shipped 2026-09-30, not yet verified on a Mac]` MLX models can be searched and downloaded from the in-app catalog too, one folder per model. `[shipped 2026-10-01, not yet verified on a Mac]`
- **Older and non-NVIDIA GPUs are found through the engine itself.** `[Preview, implemented 2026-10-08; hardware output verified on one machine only]` When `nvidia-smi` is missing from PATH, refuses to run (an old driver on a hybrid laptop such as a Surface Book 2 answers "not administrator / no TCC device"), or the GPU is AMD or Intel, the GPU probe asks the installed engine's own `llama-server --list-devices`, which names the GPUs the model will really load on. Those readings feed the same memory meters, the "will it fit" chips and the preset a finished download saves, so such a machine gets a GPU offload and context plan instead of silently loading on the CPU with a 2k context. Only discrete GPUs count: an integrated part's memory is system RAM, so it is never added to a card's VRAM, and a machine with only an integrated GPU still reads as having none. The reading carries no utilization figure (the dock names its source as `llama-server`), is cached for 15 seconds because it costs a process spawn, and a load's before/after measurement always takes a fresh one. Acceptance: with `nvidia-smi` failing and a discrete Vulkan GPU present, the library reports the GPU's memory; with neither, nothing changes. Verified: deterministic parser, vendor-priority, cache/fresh-read and recursion-safe binary-lookup tests, and a real `--list-devices` read on a GTX 1050 laptop. Discrete RX Vega 56/64 cards are retained; integrated Vega 8/11 Graphics are excluded. Registering an engine invalidates an earlier no-GPU monitor reading. The probe reads only local engine/device information, starts a hidden bounded process, makes no network request and does not install a runtime or change existing saved presets. Running-app GPU display and download-to-preset acceptance are still unverified. Tracking: [T185](TASKS.md#gpu-fallback-preview-added-2026-10-08). **Planned, not built:** a visible "GPU found but unused" notice on a CPU-only load, a Windows hint when a GPU is in an error state (restart or reseat), and AMD or Linux verification.
- **Loaded models survive the engine's own restarts.** `[shipped 2026-09-30, desktop]` The model servers and the address in front of them run in the engine daemon, so if the rest of the engine restarts, loaded models keep answering and are picked up again; the daemon's router adds about 0.1 ms per request.
- **Opening a chat never waits on a busy agent.** `[shipped 2026-10-01, desktop]` The chat list and opening a chat are served by the engine daemon straight from disk, not by the part of the engine running agents, so an agent streaming in one tab no longer holds up switching to another. With that part busy, opening a 7 MB image chat took 14 ms instead of 403 ms, and listing 301 chats 22 ms instead of 797 ms. Renaming, pinning, queuing a follow-up and the chat's other settings are saved by the daemon too, and a change made while a turn is running is kept when the turn saves.
- **The prompt cache survives between turns.** `[shipped 2026-09-30; controlled by the default-on Prompt caching setting as of 2026-10-07]` An agent resends a large system prompt, the project context and every tool result on each turn. The engine keeps each chat's cache in place on the GPU and reuses cached chunks around an edit, with a RAM prompt cache behind it for chats that lose their slot. Coming back to one of six rotating chats takes 0.8 s to the first token, against 1.3 s in Ollama and 1.6 s in LM Studio.
- **Speculative decoding by default.** `[shipped 2026-09-30]` Prompt lookup guesses upcoming tokens from text already in the conversation and the model checks them in one pass, with no second model needed. Code edits repeat much of their input, and there it runs about 4.5x the speed of Ollama or LM Studio on the same file; ordinary replies are about a third faster too. It is on by default (vision models included) and switches off per model. A small same-family draft model can be attached per model as well; it is opt-in because a draft that is too large slows ordinary replies down.
- **Sub-agents run side by side.** `[shipped]` The engine serves several requests at once from one loaded model with a shared cache pool (llama.cpp's automatic slots), instead of queueing them one after another as Ollama does by default.
- **Published, reproducible benchmarks.** `[shipped 2026-09-30]` `crates/nekko-bench` runs the same model file at the same quantization through any OpenAI-compatible server (Nekko Agent, Ollama, LM Studio, vLLM where the platform supports it) and reports time to first token, decode and prefill tokens/s, concurrent throughput, a multi-turn agent session and a six-chat rotation. Results are published with the machine they ran on, including where Nekko Agent is behind (the first turn of a brand-new long conversation is still slower than Ollama's); a claim that the numbers do not support is not made.
- **New flags only where the installed engine knows them.** The engine reads its own binary's help once and passes only the flags that build supports, so an engine installed before these changes keeps starting.

### Setting up a local model

Servers the user already runs live on **Model Providers**, under **Connected local servers**. Auto-discovery probes common localhost ports (Ollama 11434, LM Studio 1234, vLLM 8000, plus `127.0.0.1` to catch IPv4-only servers). They add or confirm a server, test the connection (green Connected / Offline), and the model list populates. Cloud providers are added the same way, grouped into **Local** vs **Cloud** sections. Favorites pin to the top of the picker.

Each local server then renders as a **runtime card**: a power toggle that really starts and stops it, its address ready to copy or edit in place, and the models it currently holds with the real split between GPU and system memory. Picking a model opens a **fit drawer**, where one context slider drives a live memory projection: weights, KV cache, and runtime overhead stacked against what the machine actually has free, with a marker where that memory runs out. Dragging the slider past what the machine can hold turns the bar amber and then pushes it past the marker, so a user learns a 64k context will spill onto the CPU *before* waiting through the load rather than after. Underneath, one plain sentence says what will happen and why ("12 of 32 layers will run on the CPU, because the KV cache alone needs 14.2 GB"), and up to three one-click fixes offer a smaller context, a cheaper KV cache, fewer parallel slots, or a smaller build. Expert controls (parallelism, KV cache type, GPU offload share, TTL) sit behind an Advanced disclosure, and settings a server only reads at startup are shown as the exact environment line to set, marked restart-required, rather than as a control that would silently not apply.

### First-agent provider setup

Implemented in the desktop renderer; desktop visual and live authentication verification remain pending. Adding a chat refreshes provider state and routes to **Model Providers** instead of creating an empty chat when no enabled provider is configured. The automatically registered Nekko Agent engine alone is not sufficient: it must have a selected or available model. Existing empty chats show an illustrated **Set up my first agent** entry instead of an empty model list. A configured but unreachable external provider remains a connection-recovery case, not a first-run reset.

The first-provider page is a short **Choose → Connect → Start** flow with an illustrated Nekko, large responsive provider buttons, bundled brand icons, plain-language account/cost descriptions, a back action, and an explicit continuation to the agent/model choice after saving. Users can instead open Model Server for the managed local path. The same large buttons replace the generic add-provider chips for existing users. Connection name and endpoint live under a disclosure; API-key and server connections are tested before saving, with failures kept on the form. Cancel never advances to success. Keyboard focus and selected states are visible; brief entry/hover motion respects reduced-motion preferences.

Privacy boundaries: icons load locally; selection alone never signs in, installs models, or saves credentials. Existing host-side subscription authentication and credential handling remain unchanged. Online-provider disclosure explains that sent messages go to that provider and paid plans/usage may apply. Acceptance verification must cover no providers, disabled providers, an empty engine, a model-ready engine, existing providers, cancellation, connection failure, successful continuation, narrow/wide layouts, both themes, and reduced motion. Automated readiness/routing and card-markup tests are included; full interactive wizard, OAuth, and desktop motion evidence are not yet verified.

### First-run setup (planned onboarding wizard)
