The first launch opens a calm, full-screen setup wizard over the app; every step can be skipped (a per-step **Skip**, plus **Skip setup**), and it never auto-shows again once dismissed (Settings can replay it). Three skippable steps:

1. **Theme.** System-match, Light, Dark, and a set of elegant tech presets, each shown as a circle filled with a gradient of its palette so the choice is visual, not a word list. Clicking previews live.
2. **Providers.** Two groups: **online** (Claude and ChatGPT lead with one-click *subscription* sign-in so a Pro/Max or Plus/Pro plan works without metered API fees, API keys remain the secondary path, plus OpenRouter and a generic OpenAI-compatible endpoint) and **local** (auto-discovery surfaces running Ollama / LM Studio / vLLM servers for one-click add, and an **Nekko Agent** card offers the app's own managed local-model path).
3. **Integrations.** Two groups: **use Nekko inside other tools**, which installs the app as an MCP subagent into detected agent CLIs (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor) with one click, and **connect your apps** (GitHub, Linear, Slack, Discord, Jira, Teams, Gmail, Drive) so workflows and context can reach them.

### Signing in with a subscription (planned)

A user with a **Claude Pro/Max** or **ChatGPT Plus/Pro/Business** subscription clicks **Sign in with Claude** / **Sign in with ChatGPT** on the Models page (or in onboarding), approves in the browser tab that opens, and lands back on a connected provider marked **Subscription**, no API key, no per-token billing. The flow uses the same public OAuth sign-in the vendor's own CLI uses; the honest caveat shown next to the button is that the vendor's terms are written around their own apps, so the subscription path is offered as-is and the API-key path remains fully supported. Headless or remote setups get a paste-the-code fallback, and anyone already signed in to the Claude Code or Codex CLI can **import** that session in one click instead of repeating the browser flow. Sign out revokes the local token; a failed refresh prompts re-auth rather than silently falling back. `[fixed 2026-10-01]` The ChatGPT callback listens on port 1455; when that port is already held, Nekko Agent closes a stale sign-in attempt of its own still parked there and retries once, and only when the holder is a stranger does it fall back to the paste flow. The manual path keeps the same redirect address (the code rides in it) and tells the user to copy the address bar of the browser's error page, which is the only place that URL appears, rather than hunt for a code that page does not show.

### One-click setup for safe offline PC control (planned)

**The experience:** a user who does not want to assemble an AI stack opens **Set up offline PC control**. Nekko inspects their machine, explains which models and runtimes fit, and shows the download size, disk/memory budget, supported actions, and permissions required. One **Set up** action then downloads or imports, verifies, installs, configures, and checks the complete local stack. Separate operating-system permission dialogs and license acceptance still require the user's approval; one click never means silently granting access or bypassing security controls.

The stack includes a local **intent LLM** that turns a request into a bounded, structured action plan, **TTS** for spoken responses, **STT** when voice input is enabled, and any supported runtime or computer-control adapter those components require. Text input and captions remain available; push-to-talk is the initial voice path rather than always-on listening. Audio and captured screen content are local and ephemeral by default. Local spoken intent is a request to evaluate, not permission to run arbitrary generated shell commands.

**Hardware-aware selection:** check OS/version, CPU architecture and supported instruction/backend features, total and available RAM, GPU/backend/driver compatibility, dedicated VRAM versus unified memory, and available installation disk space. Compare those facts with versioned, verified model/runtime requirements, supported languages, and required intent/STT/TTS capabilities. Fit the whole concurrently loaded stack, including runtime overhead, context/KV memory, and headroom for the user's normal applications. Do not double-count unified memory, assume separate GPUs form one pool, or equate a small download with a usable runtime footprint. Distinguish minimum compatibility from a recommendation expected to feel responsive. Validate with a short on-device warm-up and benchmark; present measured readiness rather than inventing performance claims.

If the recommended stack does not fit, offer a smaller supported model, reduced concurrency, or a tested CPU-only path with its trade-offs. If none meets the minimum requirements, say exactly what is missing and keep setup blocked. Unknown hardware support is **unverified**, not a pass. Never silently install an incompatible model or switch to a cloud provider. Users can review or override recommendations only within supported combinations and keep using an existing compatible runtime rather than being forced to replace it.

**Computer control:** supported local actions should include opening an approved application, switching to an approved window, adjusting a supported local setting, and entering text into a confirmed target. Scope access by app/window and action, prefer semantic/accessibility APIs over blind coordinates, and show the intended action before execution. Recheck the target immediately before acting and stop if focus, permissions, or observed state changed. Ambiguous voice recognition or low-confidence intent triggers clarification, not a guess. Treat text seen in applications, documents, and web pages as untrusted content, never as authorization to override the user's request.

**Safety contract:** a deterministic action broker validates the model's structured proposal against granted capabilities. No unrestricted shell or elevated permissions by default. Sending messages, purchases, destructive changes, credential/security changes, and other sensitive actions need explicit, action-specific confirmation; a model response or background audio cannot approve itself. Provide an always-visible stop button and keyboard shortcut, interruptible action boundaries, bounded execution, clear failure/partial-completion reporting, and a local activity log with sensitive content redacted. Undo is offered only for actions with a real, verified inverse; do not promise universal rollback. Revoking a permission stops related queued actions. These controls reduce risk but are not a guarantee that a model can never make a mistake.

**Offline means end to end:** initial provisioning needs internet access for approved downloads unless the user imports a verified offline bundle. Once ready, intent recognition, speech input, speech output, and supported PC actions run locally with no account, cloud fallback, remote telemetry, or network access required. Online actions are unavailable in this mode. Verify the experience with external network access blocked, not merely by pointing chat at localhost. A local server capable of proxying to the cloud is not automatically a verified offline runtime.

**Availability and boundaries:** current hardware telemetry, file/shell tools, model discovery, and guardrails are reusable foundations, not an implemented voice/desktop-control stack. The current chat **Offline** toggle disables all tools; this feature needs its own explicit local-only action policy and must not be implemented by weakening that existing toggle. Build a platform/action support matrix for macOS, Windows, and Linux and expose unsupported OS capabilities honestly. The **requirements evaluator now exists** (AN9b): `machineReadiness` probes the machine (OS/arch, provable CPU features, backends, per-device VRAM or unified memory, RAM, free disk) and evaluates it against a versioned, curated catalog of runtime/intent/STT/TTS components, returning `recommended`/`compatible`/`insufficient`/`unverified` per role with structured reasons and a combined concurrently-resident budget; an unprobed fact yields `unverified`, never a guess. Still to build: automatic provisioning (AN9c), the voice pipeline (AN9d), the permission-scoped control broker (AN9e), the setup/control UI that surfaces this report (AN9f), and end-to-end safety/offline verification.

### Subagent transcript identity

`[implemented and renderer verified on 2026-10-06]` Delegation in the desktop/shared web transcript carries a fork/branch icon alongside an agent icon, with the child task title and subagent count visible even when working steps are collapsed. Expanded persisted delegation steps distinguish **To subagent** (the task) from **From subagent** (the matching tool result), including an explicit failure or missing-result label. Ordinary tools and assistant narration are not relabelled as child replies. Agent-watch continuation prompts are shown as watch rows, not subagent replies (see *Watch rows and subagent models*); prefix recognition is a presentation hint, not authenticated provenance.

Acceptance: collapsed and expanded delegation remain distinguishable in light/dark themes and narrow/wide transcript panes; request/result association uses tool-call IDs; labels convey meaning without relying on color. No credentials, additional remote calls, routing changes or permission changes are introduced. The existing watch/task text remains intact. Native mobile transcript parity and authenticated watch provenance metadata are not implemented by this change. Automated component/transcript checks verify labels and association. Isolated full-chat Electron checks cover completed, failed and missing results, ordinary prompt controls, watch labels and expanded-state retention through virtualization. Matched wide/narrow light/dark captures and sampled expand/collapse frames were directly inspected.

### Watch rows and subagent models

`[implemented 2026-10-15; unit-tested and verified in an isolated hidden renderer fixture (dark/light, wide/narrow) against the base revision; not yet checked in the packaged app]` Durable agent watches read like the other agent rows instead of chat messages. A step that arms a watch shows a binoculars icon, what it watches and what fires it (**Watching PR #555 · wakes on any PR change or after 1h**, or **Timer watch · wakes after 10 min**), and a subline **then · …** with the first sentence of its continuation. A chip beside it is live: time left (counting down each second while visible), **overdue N min** in the warning colour once a deadline passes by more than a minute without waking, **due · waiting for the chat** when it fired but the chat is busy, **retrying** after polling errors, then **triggered**, **cancelled** or **failed to wake**. A watch that could not be armed says **Watch not armed** with the reason. The wake-up the host sends is no longer a user bubble: it is one row, **Watch triggered · PR #555 changed (merged, checks pending)** or **Deadline reached**, with its time and the same then-line; expanding it shows the full wake-up text. This replaces the earlier **Agent watch · automated wake-up** label. Prefix recognition remains a presentation hint, not authenticated provenance.

Watch state comes from a new read-only `session:watches` channel listing a chat's watches (kind, PR URL, status, deadline, pending, failures, last error), never their continuation prompts, which the transcript already holds in the arming call. Rows refresh on the chat's tool results, turn ends and messages, plus a 15 s poll while one is on screen.

Every subagent row (group header, each step, standalone card) shows the model it runs on as a quiet chip, with the full ID in its tooltip and where it came from: the child chat's own model, else the call's `model_id`, else the saved delegation route or this chat's model when the call named none. The Working subagents panel shows it after the child's title. The running dots now sit before a live row's text, in a fixed box, so neither the dots nor the text move as the third dot lands.

### One copy of the reply being written

`[implemented 2026-10-15, verified in an isolated hidden renderer fixture, light and dark]` While a reply is in flight the chat draws the current turn once, from the live run under the transcript. The transcript itself stops at the prompt that started the turn, even though the daemon checkpoints the chat after every tool result and the pane re-reads it: that checkpointed tail (the turn's narration and tool calls so far) is the same content the live run is drawing. It used to be drawn twice, once as a settled bubble with a Done row above the working steps and once streaming below them, until the real answer landed. Once the turn ends and the persisted reply has replaced the held live copy, the transcript shows the full turn as before.

Acceptance: during a turn the narration appears once, in the live region, with no Done row; after the turn the persisted copy appears once in the transcript. No host, daemon or storage change; the packaged app and native OS chrome were not captured.

### Sleeping chats and image-bearing replies

`[implemented; verification in progress on 2026-10-07]` An idle chat with an active durable watch displays **Sleeping · will check in N mins** instead of Done, using hours for waits over one hour. The host exposes only the earliest active watch deadline, not its continuation prompt. Running replies, errors, approvals and questions take precedence. Watch cancellation, delivery and restart are reflected by event refresh plus bounded polling. PR watches may wake before their deadline. Sleeping is informational: the user can send a new reply without cancelling the watch or changing permissions. Since 2026-10-12 the engine daemon owns the watches (`agent-watches.json`, its single writer), arms them from a turn it drives, serves the deadline, and runs the scheduler; the app still reads a PR's state (`gh pr view`) and continues the chat for it, until those move to the daemon too.

The local daemon accepts `loop:run` requests up to 64 MiB, including retained transcript images; other RPC requests retain the 2 MiB limit. The host rejects larger reply payloads explicitly rather than silently discarding history or attachments. Acceptance: an image-bearing request above the previous 2 MiB limit starts successfully, oversized requests remain bounded, and replying in a sleeping chat remains available. No new remote calls or permissions are granted. Focused Rust/host tests and isolated synthetic full-renderer interactions cover these behaviors; live provider/image conversation recovery and native mobile verification remain unverified.

Delegation presentation now uses a left-aligned, borderless branch-and-agent section with **Spawned subagent · task summary**. Expanding preserves the full task and matching result, including failure/missing-result labels. Multi-step groups retain counts; single delegation groups surface the task directly. Existing transcript ID association and permission/privacy boundaries remain unchanged. Matched component before/after evidence covers wide/narrow light/dark layouts; full-renderer interactions also check persisted expansion.

### Delegating frontier planning to a local worker

The lead agent can delegate a scoped task through `spawn_agent` with optional `provider_id` and `model_id` targets. Leaving both out preserves the parent's model. Changing provider requires a known exact model ID; the host checks the configured, enabled target and its model list before creating the child. Unknown, unavailable, disabled, or known non-chat models produce an error, never a silent fallback to a different or paid model. The child appears in the existing nested session UI with its selected provider/model and inherits the parent's execution policy and disabled tools. Ask mode and the ask-everything sandbox require approval before starting a sub-agent or calling an MCP tool, so indirect execution cannot bypass that boundary.

This first implementation is **explicit cross-provider delegation**, not automatic price/capability/resource optimization. The lead receives enabled provider IDs and labels, not credentials, and must ask for a known model rather than invent an ID. An opt-in local-worker default and a routing-decision UI remain planned. Incognito delegation is blocked because child sessions are persisted; it must not quietly retain an incognito task.

The existing **Offline** chat mode is deliberately narrower than normal local-agent work: it disables tools and connectors. The host now also rejects cloud or non-loopback provider endpoints before making a request, prevents unoffered tool execution, skips connector fetching in offline context previews, and suppresses hidden post-turn spec refreshes. This is endpoint-policy enforcement, not an operating-system network sandbox; users still control whether their local inference server proxies requests elsewhere. Broader independent model actions, including explicit spec generation, need the same privacy audit before claiming application-wide network isolation.

### Working in a codebase

The user wires one or more **folders** into a chat (multi-root), choosing one primary folder and any number of supporting folders in the Context Inspector's Folders section. The primary is also picked from a folder menu at the start of the composer's bottom strip (project folders, No folder, Add folder…); switching or clearing it keeps the old primary as supporting, and the composer's + menu Folder attaches the new folder to the chat. The workspace gets indexed (file tree + code outline + search), with index search and workspace memory spanning the selected folders while the agent's working directory remains the primary. They talk to the model in a unified **Chat** surface, messages render as speech bubbles, and reasoning appears above each answer as a collapsed summary such as "Thought for 3s" (expandable to inspect the reasoning). A multi-step turn's working steps (reasoning, tool calls, and inter-tool narration) fold into a single collapsible **activity group** ("Worked on 6 steps · read_file, grep") so a long run reads as one expandable line instead of a wall of "Used `<tool>`" rows; only the final answer stays a bubble. Risky commands trigger an approval prompt. Each user prompt reveals a subtle on-hover action row (its timestamp, Copy, Edit & resend, and **Reset here**, which rewinds the chat to that message and re-runs it), replacing the old single Regenerate button. If a local model returns an empty response mid-loop, the reply retries once with a nudge and, failing that, surfaces a clear marker instead of stalling silently. If a model instead **degenerates into repeating itself**, the reply is cut off the moment the output collapses into a cycle, keeping everything it wrote before the loop and saying plainly what happened and what usually helps, rather than streaming the same sentence for minutes. A live subtext under the conversation tracks the in-flight reply (elapsed, tokens/sec, tokens generated) and keeps a last-reply summary when idle. **Tokens/sec is decode throughput**, output tokens over the time the model actually spent generating them, summed across the reply's steps: it deliberately excludes prompt processing, tool runs, and approval waits, so it matches the figure the local runtime reports rather than being dragged down by however long the turn happened to take (Ollama's own `eval_duration` is used where it's offered; other providers are timed from first token to the usage report). Single-digit rates keep a decimal, since 8.6 and 9.4 tok/s are different answers to "is this model fast enough". The UI says **reply** for a chat exchange and **iteration** for one cycle of a long-running run; "turn" is gone from the surface. Markdown renders on both sides of the conversation, so a dashed list typed into the composer comes back as real bullets rather than raw dashes. The composer accepts pasted images and has a **+** menu for a photo, file, or folder; attachments sit in a thumbnail strip inside the composer card, and right-clicking any chat image offers Copy or Save. Images are sent to vision-capable models as multimodal messages, with larger previews and a full-size lightbox on click. The lightbox closes when clicking anywhere outside the photo, including blank dialog space, and has a large, visible Close button as well as Escape dismissal. Invoking a `/skill` adds a compact usage chip while keeping the user's own input separate; the full skill instructions still go to the model. Above the composer, a two-row instrument strip separates **how this agent runs** (mode, tools, and the Offline / Incognito privacy switches on the right) from **which brain it uses** (model, thinking, effort). Automate sits at the top right of the composer with a warning-colored lightning icon, and the context readout counts the draft as you type it, in the strip and in the Context Inspector alike. Per-chat control modes (Ask / Guardrails / YOLO, plus Offline and Incognito) govern how much it confirms. Once a reply lands, the same model that wrote it suggests what the user probably wants to say next: a few short follow-ups appear as one-click chips above the composer (clicking one sends it outright), and the single likeliest next message appears as ghost text in the empty composer that **Right Arrow** types in. The suggestions ride a small sideband call that persists nothing, vanish the moment a new turn starts, and simply stay away on failure.

### In-chat pull requests

Updated 2026-10-02. Open pull-request cards lead with the actual GitHub head branch, with repository, PR number and target branch retained as secondary context. Until live metadata arrives, the label uses the repository and PR number from the URL rather than inventing a branch. Open and closed cards share the merged banner's compact rounded layout with a faint neutral wash, no enclosing border, and slim borderless actions. Approve keeps a green text cue, Decline keeps a danger cue, and Merge uses neutral ink; filled gradients and purple are reserved for the unchanged merged celebration. Existing action confirmation and disabled states remain intact.

Reading files or searching code does not create PR cards from example URLs in tests or documentation. Explicitly discussed PR links and shell output, including `gh pr create`, still surface cards.

### Completion PR verification follow-up

Updated 2026-10-02. Fallback shell commands preserve quoted arguments and stop the shell's descendants on cancellation on both Windows and Unix. Unix launches create a separate process group using a launch API that honors that option. Completion UI verification includes desktop/mobile web screenshots in both themes and a local motion recording; native iOS/Android visual validation and GitHub media upload remain outstanding.

### Editing, reviewing & previewing, without leaving the app

Files open as windows right in the workspace: a **Files** explorer window lists a project root and opens files as windows of their own beside the chat and terminal, and the app-wide Folders panel does the same from the right-hand side. Markdown is rendered or code opens in a mono editor, with an integrated browser pane for the live preview beside the code. When the agent edits files, a **Changes** panel shows a line diff and the user keeps or reverts at line/file/all granularity. Reviewing AI-made changes, browsing a preview, and writing better prompts (via the always-on prompt analyzer) all happen in one calm window. *(Planned next:)* the user drops a **+ comment** on a line and either queues it for the next prompt or runs it immediately, turning in-place review notes into agent work.

On desktop, **Commands** in a chat opens an optional read-only terminal window showing the agent's approved shell commands and their output as it arrives. It mirrors the existing guarded execution rather than running a command twice. Desktop chats can also ask the agent to use a browser: by default a hidden in-app Nekko browser that Playwright drives inside the app's own Chromium (see *In-app browser automation*), or, explicitly, an existing browser started with localhost CDP enabled, attached through Stagehand. Every browser action requires approval; attaching never silently exposes or takes over an ordinary browser profile. In both modes the browser tool navigates, inspects page text, clicks, and fills a selected element; the in-app browser adds waiting, typing, key presses, scrolling, text and link extraction, page expressions, screenshots into the chat, tabs and a console/network log. It is unavailable in web, cloud, offline, and unattended runs. The integrated preview browser remains a separate, user-driven pane.

### Designing: draw it or describe it (Design tab)

The **Design** tab is where designs get made, a Claude Design-style surface with two ways in, both ending as a working prototype:

1. **Draw it.** A pen-and-touch-first sketch canvas (built to be used from an iPad or iPhone via the web edition: pointer events, Apple Pencil friendly, palm-safe `touch-action`). The user sketches boxes, arrows, and labels, adds an optional note ("mobile app, dark theme"), and hits **Generate prototype**: a vision model turns the sketch into a polished, self-contained HTML code prototype.
2. **Describe it.** Write a prompt ("a landing page for a sushi delivery startup") and watch a design pop up. Starter idea chips help the first prompt along.

Either way the concept lands on the board as a card and is **mirrored into the workspace as a real HTML file** (`nekko-designs/`, or an existing earlier-brand folder), so the user, or any agent, keeps iterating on it as code. Selecting a concept opens a side sheet with a **Refine** box ("make the hero darker", "add a pricing section") that regenerates the design in place, follow-up by follow-up: the user stays the designer, the model just drafts fast.

The board still holds **live snapshots** of the app's real pages. The user pins **notes** on a page or leaves a **comment** that they Add-to-prompt or Run-now. As the agent reworks the UI, the snapshots refresh live and an "updating" badge marks the page being changed; clicking it jumps to the agent doing the work. The board makes "what does my app look like, and what should change" a visual, direct-manipulation surface.

### Seeing exactly what the model gets (Context Inspector)

A toggleable right-side panel makes context legible. **Sources** lists the folders wired in, attached files, the chat's `SPEC.md` (with Build-from-chat / Live controls), and **Guidelines**: detected guideline files (AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md) plus memory entries, each with its own enable/disable control. Guidelines sits above the Spec panel; the Spec panel separately offers creation of missing project instruction files rather than showing a second Guidelines section. New chats open without waiting for a full reload of every saved transcript. **Breakdown** is a live view of exactly what's entering the prompt on the next reply (files, guidelines, memory snippets, connector data, index snippets), each item toggleable/pinnable with token counts, plus a context-window headroom bar. Workspace cards refresh estimated transcript use every four seconds while a chat is running. At 95% usage, the chat warns the user and automatically compacts after the current reply finishes. Compaction summarizes older conversation in the same session and shows its progress; while it runs, the user can send the summary to a new chat instead or stop compacting, leaving the original transcript unchanged. `[updated 2026-10-03]` `[updated 2026-10-01]` Between the capacity panel and the resource monitors sits a collapsible **Model settings** group: each model the engine holds in memory, where it runs (GPU, split, or CPU, with the layer count), its footprint and context, and, folded under one click, the settings it runs with (GPU offload, context, KV cache, keep-loaded) and a **Reload** that applies them in place. It answers "is my model on the GPU" beside the chat that is asking, above the meters that would report the consequence, and it exists only while an engine is installed.

### Living spec from a conversation

Any chat can synthesize a `SPEC.md` in its workspace from the conversation (using the session's own provider/model, one-shot). With **Live** on, the spec is rebuilt after every turn so it tracks design changes and new features as the conversation evolves.

### Training a model while you watch (Training tab)

The user opens **Training**, says what the model is for ("classify support tickets by urgency"), points at a dataset (Hugging Face, Kaggle, a local path, or a URL) and optionally a base model, and hits Start. That's the whole simple path; an **Expert levers** panel exposes the full configuration (framework, epochs, batch size, learning rate, metric + direction, experiment/time budgets, free-form constraints) for people who want control. A **data-scientist agent** then does the real work in a dedicated chat session, and the dashboard shows its progression live: stat tiles (best score, experiments, success rate, emergent niches, self-repairs, runtime), a **current champion** card, and the **idea maze**, a growing tree of every idea the agent tried, color-coded (tried / got better / best so far / running) with the path to the leader highlighted. The user can **guide the agent mid-run**: hints, new training approaches, or corrections fold into its next turn. Starting a run drops the user straight onto its live dashboard, where a "now" strip spells out what's happening (from "starting up…" through the currently-running experiment), and an **Artifacts card** makes the output concrete: it lists exactly what the run built (the trained model plus each harness file), where each lives, and how to use it, with one-click open in the OS file manager. The distinctive twist: a run can also produce the **harness for the model's use case**, an agent file (AGENTS.md), a skill, and/or a spec, so the output isn't just weights, it's a ready-to-use capability.

### Automating repeatable work (Workflows tab)

Some work isn't a conversation, it's a **procedure that should run the same way every time**, often without anyone watching. The **Workflows** tab is that surface, modelled on GitHub Actions but with the steps a coding agent actually needs: a step is an **agent prompt**, an installed **skill**, another **workflow**, or a **shell command**.

The part that makes it more than a script runner is that steps are **routed rather than merely sequenced**. Every step declares where the run goes when it succeeds and when it fails, and a route can point **back at an earlier step**, so the natural shape of real work is expressible: build, then verify, and when verify finds problems, go back to build and try again. Loops are bounded (a step can be re-entered a fixed number of times) so a workflow that never converges still stops and says so, rather than running forever. Steps also carry **retries**, a **timeout**, and a **continue-on-error** flag. Agent steps report their own outcome with a PASS/FAIL token, which is what lets an agent step be routed the way a shell step's exit code is; a run's agent steps all share one chat session, so a verify step sees what the build step actually did.

A workflow starts from whatever fires it: **by hand**, on a **schedule** (a cron expression or a plain interval), from the **CLI** (`nekko-agent workflow run <name>`, or `trigger <command>` to fire everything listening for it), from **Slack** (a channel, optionally filtered to messages containing a word), or from a **git provider event**, a pull request opened, updated, closed, merged, commented on or reviewed, a push, a tag, an issue, filtered by repo and branch. Provider events are **normalized across GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket and other**, so a workflow reacts to "a PR opened" without caring whose vocabulary it was. One workflow can carry several triggers.

The tab is built for an install that accumulates **hundreds** of these, and written for people who have never made one. A collapsible **How workflows work** strip defines the vocabulary in place: an automation is the whole thing (steps plus triggers), a manual trigger is the Run button or the CLI (every workflow can run by hand even with its other triggers off), a scheduled job is a cron or interval trigger, and event triggers are a pull request, a Slack message, a connector poll, or a webhook. A left rail filters by search (which reaches into step bodies, not just names), enabled state, category, and trigger kind, and the trigger filter is grouped by family — **you start it** (manual, CLI), **on a schedule**, **on an event** — with each kind carrying its own explanation. The list groups either **by category** ("what do I have?") or **by trigger** ("what happens when a PR opens?"), with a workflow appearing under every event it listens for. A row shows its state, step count, triggers, and how the last run went; expanding it reveals the **step canvas**, a node graph in the same visual language as the Skills tab, where failure routes are drawn in the danger color and a loop back to an earlier step dips under the row, so a retry loop is something you see rather than something you read. The same canvas doubles as the live run view, each node wearing that run's outcome, beside the attempt-by-attempt run log (with a link into any step's transcript) and that workflow's run history. Workflows are created either **from scratch** (a blank, run-by-hand automation) or **from an example** (a build/verify loop, a PR reviewer, a nightly maintenance sweep, a local CI runner), and the picker presents those as two visibly different starting points rather than one undifferentiated list; the editor's canvas redraws as you type and flags steps nothing routes to and validates a cron expression against its next fire. Workflows can be duplicated, enabled/disabled, and deleted.

### Handing the agent a long-running goal

The user gives the agent **something to get finished** ("ship the CSV import end to end: parser, validation, tests, docs"), optionally with context and constraints, and the agent works it **plan-first** in three phases: **Plan** (it studies the goal and the workspace, then writes an ordered execution plan of concrete, verifiable steps), **Execute** (it works the plan step by step, marking each step active and then done, with a one-line outcome note, only once verified), and **Iterate** (when reality disagrees, a step fails, new work surfaces, it revises the plan and keeps going until every step is done and the goal is verifiably met). This is deliberately **not a model-testing surface** (that's the Training tab); it's a "work until finished" surface for real work.

The dashboard reads as **mission control, not a chat**: a big **"working for"** clock, live status plus a **phase chip** (planning / executing / verifying / finished), and the **live plan checklist** front and center, each step with its status glyph, note, and a progress bar, alongside a current-step card, goal-shaped stat tiles (plan progress, iterations, attempts, runtime), a **Mission control** card that surfaces the budgets the loop enforces (iteration budget, time budget), and a prominent activity feed of plan milestones, failures, and self-repairs. Course-correcting is a full-width **"Steer the mission"** console (deliberately one nudge at a time, folded into the next iteration, not a back-and-forth chat); the underlying agent transcript is still reachable via a low-key **Transcript** link. Under the hood a goal is **not just one ever-growing chat**: each iteration runs on a **bounded context window** (the last few turns; the brief re-injects the plan every turn), and the loop is **guaranteed to terminate**, the host enforces a turn budget and any time budget, and **detects a stall** (no measurable plan/experiment progress plus a repeating answer) to pause the run and ask for a hint rather than looping for thousands of iterations. Runs survive restarts and resume automatically; resuming a budget-capped run grants a fresh leg.

### Driving your home model from your phone (remote control)

The user opens **Settings → Remote access**, hits Enable (the managed relay is prefilled; a self-hosted relay URL works the same), and taps **Pair a device**: a QR appears carrying a **single-use pairing code that expires in 10 minutes**. They scan it from the phone (the Nekko Agent app, the phone's camera app, which opens the Nekko Agent app, or any browser) and the phone shows up under **Paired devices** with a live connection dot. A phone browser gets the app against the home machine, limited to what a paired phone may do (`[updated 2026-10-14]`, see "What a paired phone may do"): chats, approvals and questions work; training runs, goals, the Command Center, terminals and most settings are refused by the computer. The native app is the focused version of the same thing (next section). Inference and tool calls execute locally under the machine's guardrails/sandbox.

Security is layered: the local agent dials out to the relay (no inbound ports), every payload is end-to-end encrypted (the relay routes only ciphertext, so relayed local-model use is inherently zero-data-retention), and on top of the transport every device must pass a handshake against the machine's **device registry**: unknown devices are denied unless they present a live pairing code, each device can be **renamed or revoked** (revocation kicks it mid-connection), and **Rotate secret** is the cryptographic kill switch that unpairs everything. Remote access survives restarts; the agent reconnects on boot. Full guide: [docs/REMOTE.md](docs/REMOTE.md).

#### Finding the computer and choosing the transport (decided 2026-10-12)

The QR stays the universal pairing path, but on a home network it should rarely be needed. The desktop **advertises itself** with mDNS / DNS-SD (Bonjour on macOS and iOS, NSD on Android, an mDNS responder in the Windows and Linux builds) as `_nekko-agent._tcp`, carrying only a display name, the relay URL and a pairing-session hint, never a secret. The phone's Computers tab lists advertised computers on the same Wi-Fi and, when Tailscale is on, on the tailnet (unicast DNS-SD over the tailnet, the same mechanism OpenClaw uses), so pairing becomes "tap the computer, approve on the desktop". The single-use pairing code and the device registry handshake are unchanged; discovery only removes the paste.

Transport policy follows one rule, copied from the strictest competitor: **plain `ws://` is accepted only toward private ranges** (loopback, RFC 1918, link-local, and Tailscale's `100.64.0.0/10` CGNAT range), and the desktop refuses to generate a pairing link with a plaintext URL that points anywhere else. Everything public is `wss://`. The end-to-end layer applies in all cases; the rule protects the transport-auth query string and keeps a misconfigured relay from leaking metadata. The desktop can run the relay itself for the Tailscale case ("Relay on this computer"), which is the free, nothing-hosted default; a hosted relay is for phones that are not on the user's network.

#### Hosted relay tiers, free notifications and what the server may know (decided 2026-10-12)

The hosted relay (nekkoagent.com, or a self-hosted copy of the same image) has two tiers plus the self-hosted option, in the spirit of OpenClaw's free push relay and Happy Coder's free sync server, but with the traffic leg metered so it never has to be switched off:

- **Push is free for everyone.** Content-free "your run finished / needs you" notifications through the hosted relay cost nothing, including for users whose traffic rides Tailscale or their own relay: the desktop registers for push only, sends the content-free `notify` frame, and the relay forwards it to APNs / FCM. No account needed; the device token is the only state.
- **Free traffic tier, limited and fair-queued.** Without a Cloud account the hosted relay accepts a paired room with a request budget per device per day (a tunable; start around a few hundred requests and a frame-byte cap sized for chat, not file sync), one computer and two devices per room, and a **fair-share scheduler**: when the machine is busy, free rooms are throttled first and evenly (token bucket per room refilled from a shared pool), paid rooms keep their rate. Hitting a limit degrades gracefully: the phone shows "free relay busy / daily budget used, resumes at …" and offers Tailscale or Cloud; nothing is silently dropped mid-run. Limits are enforced per room and device, never by reading content.
- **Cloud (paid, or run your own).** A Cloud account lifts the budget and device limits, keeps rate under load, enables several computers per account and the ZDR / history features already specified. The same image, with `NEKKO_RELAY_ALLOW_UNAUTHENTICATED=1` or an authz URL of their own, gives self-hosters the paid behaviour for free, forever. The OSS app still never license-checks; the relay answers with the plan's allowance and the app just displays it.

**Server analytics, PII-free by construction.** The hosted relay keeps enough to run the service and to learn where it is used and for what, and nothing that identifies a person or a conversation. What is counted: connections, rooms and devices per day, app platform and version, desktop OS and version, coarse geography from the connecting IP (country or region, resolved at the edge and then discarded, never the IP), relay-side event names that are already content-free (run finished, approval needed, question asked, push sent), rate-limit hits and tier, and the opaque `kind` of a session as the desktop volunteers it (chat, task, training, goal) if the user leaves the **"Share anonymous usage"** switch on. Never kept: IPs, device tokens beyond their push purpose, room codes in analytics, message bodies, prompts, model names tied to a user, file names, folder paths, timestamps finer than the hour tied to a device. Device and room identifiers are hashed with a daily rotating salt before they reach the analytics store, so a day's active-device count is possible and a cross-day trail is not. Analytics are aggregated on the relay and shipped as counters, not rows; the raw connection log rotates within 24 hours. The desktop and phone show exactly what is sent, with one switch to turn it off, and turning it off changes no feature. This is the whole list, and the website states it in the same words.

### Native phone implementation decision (2026-10-07)

**Target:** actual platform-specific apps: SwiftUI on iOS first, Kotlin/Jetpack Compose on Android second. The existing Expo/React Native app is a migration reference, not the target implementation. Preserve it until native parity is verified. The pocket-app behavior below remains the acceptance target, not a claim that the new SwiftUI app already supports it.

**Foundation implemented; simulator verification passed:** macOS CI passed five Foundation tests, a simulator build, two Keychain/host-binding tests and a first-run UI test with ad hoc signing. Full-height light/dark simulator captures were inspected. Live authenticated-host and physical-device acceptance remain unverified.  apps/ios contains a SwiftUI read-only host client and a Foundation protocol/test package, XcodeGen app definition and macOS build/test workflow. The user explicitly connects to a user-configured HTTPS origin using a host bearer; system TLS trust and redirect rejection prevent silent insecure routing. Bearer is saved only in device-only Keychain, bound to its origin; no automatic connection on launch, no provider settings/API-key fetch or persistence. Disconnect clears in-memory connection/chat state; Forget also removes the saved bearer and origin. This limited direct-host development path does not implement or replace the end-to-end encrypted relay boundary. Sending, pairing, notifications and phone inference are unavailable and labeled as such.

**Acceptance/release gates:** real authenticated-host integration, native iPad screenshots, VoiceOver/Dynamic Type/lifecycle tests and physical-device testing remain required. This Windows implementation is not marked shipped. Then implement relay crypto parity and pairing/revocation, streaming/reconciliation and exact approvals/questions before enabling send, and finally offline Metal inference and signed TestFlight distribution. Android follows using the same fixture-backed wire, not a web wrapper. See TASKS.md for ordered tasks and credential/device dependencies.

### Native Android app (2026-10-11)

**User intent:** use the phone to run chats on the models that live on the user's computer, from a real Kotlin / Jetpack Compose app (applicationId `com.nekko.native.android`, minSdk 28, targetSdk 36), not a web wrapper and not the Expo app. Started in parallel with the iOS migration because the Android client was assigned separately; it talks to the same relay and host as every other client, so nothing on the computer changes.

**Scope of this version `[implemented; JVM, integration and build checks verified; not yet run on an emulator or device]`:**

- **Pairing:** scan the computer's pairing QR with the Google code scanner (runs inside Play services, so the app never asks for the camera permission), paste the link, or open a `nekko-agent-pair:` link from the system camera. The phone enrolls with the one-time code, which it forgets as soon as the computer welcomes it. Several computers can be paired; one is connected at a time; rename, switch and forget are on the Computers screen.
- **Remote chat, end to end:** the computer's chat list (top-level, unarchived) with Running / Needs you badges; new chats on any enabled provider, model and folder of the computer; replies streamed live; Stop; prompts sent mid-run are queued like on the desktop; the exact tool call behind an approval (command or input) with Allow / Deny; the agent's `ask_user` questions as tap-to-answer choices with a free-text "Something else". Transcripts are re-read after each turn and after a reconnect, so a phone that slept catches up.
- **Model choice comes from the computer** `[implemented 2026-10-13; verified on an emulator against a sandbox agent; not yet against the desktop daemon or the managed relay]`: the phone has no models of its own. *New chat* asks every enabled provider on the computer for its models in parallel (`models:list`, 6 s per provider like the desktop picker) and shows them in one list grouped by provider, the computer's starred models first, with search once the list is long. It opens on the computer's default model, else the model of its most recent chat, else a starred one; `Auto` is never offered. A provider that fails or times out shows *Couldn't list its models* with Retry, and one that answers with nothing says so, instead of the whole screen reading "No models found". Nothing about the models is stored on the phone.
- **Theme follows the computer** `[implemented 2026-10-13; verified on an emulator against a sandbox nekkod: live light/dark/accent changes and the phone-side override; not yet against the TS-only editions on a device, nor the managed relay]`: user intent is "settings in sync with the PC, starting from dark mode", step 1. *Settings → Appearance* on the phone offers **Same as computer** (default), **Phone setting**, **Light**, **Dark**, stored on the phone only. With *Same as computer* the app uses the computer's mode (a computer on System, or one not heard from yet, falls back to the phone's setting) and its accent for buttons, selection and, in light mode, the user's bubble. The computer announces changes live with the relayed `appearance:updated` event (`appearanceOf` in packages/shared/src/themes.ts, `appearance_of` in crates/nekkod/src/settings.rs, held equal by the relay golden): only `theme`, `themePreset` (shape-checked id) and the two accents (`#rrggbb`) leave the computer, never any other setting; the phone also reads the same fields from `settings:get` when it connects. Planned next: other shared settings both ways (default model, chat mode, effort), each opted in explicitly; preset surface colours (Nebula, Terminal and so on) are not drawn on the phone yet, only their mode and accent.
- **App lock** `[implemented 2026-10-14; verified on an API 37 emulator with a screen-lock PIN (lock on open and on return, PIN unlock, cancel, turn-off confirmation, blank recents, no-screen-lock fallback); fingerprint/face, Android 9–12 and physical devices not yet verified]`: user intent is "someone who finds the phone must not be able to use it to cause trouble; ask every time the app opens". The app is locked on every cold start and every time it comes back to the foreground (ProcessLifecycleOwner ON_STOP, which already ignores rotations). Unlocking uses the system prompt only (`BiometricPrompt`, strong biometrics or the screen lock PIN/pattern/password; weak biometrics or the screen lock on Android 9–10); Nekko never sees or stores a credential. While locked: a full-screen lock screen covers the app, the content underneath is hidden from accessibility and gets no touches or focus, so nothing can be read, answered or approved; after a cold start the relay connection does not start until the first unlock. Deep links still route underneath and show after unlocking. The setting (*Settings → Security → App lock*, its own section file after Appearance) is on by default and stored on the phone in `PhonePrefs` (`app_lock`); turning it off requires the same authentication. While it is on the window is `FLAG_SECURE`, so the recent-apps preview is blank and screenshots and screen recording are blocked (`setRecentsScreenshotEnabled(false)` alone was tried and still showed the live app in the overview on API 37). The QR scanner and the system security settings are announced as short trips out (2 min) and do not lock on return. A phone with no screen lock cannot be locked: the app opens normally and Settings shows a warning with a link to the system security settings. Not a defense against a compromised/rooted phone, or against someone who knows the screen lock; revoking the phone on the computer remains the response to a lost phone. Limits to the computer side (what a paired phone may call) are a separate change.
- **New chat defaults on the phone** `[implemented 2026-10-14; verified on an API 37 emulator against two sandbox agents (default computer at start, phone model preselected over the computer's default, missing model falls back with a notice, forgetting a computer clears its defaults); not yet on a physical device]`: user intent is "default computer and model for new chats", chosen on the phone. *Settings → New chats* has **Computer** (the paired computer the app connects to when it starts; default *The one used last*, the previous behaviour; only offered with two or more computers) and **Model on <active computer>** (a model from that computer's providers; default *Same as the computer's default*). Both are stored on the phone in `PhonePrefs` (`newChat.computer`, `newChat.models`, one model per computer, keyed by the phone's computer id); nothing is written to the computer, whose own default model is unchanged. New chat opens on the phone's model for the active computer when that computer still offers it, else on the computer's default, else its latest chat's model, as before. When the phone's model is no longer offered (provider off, model removed), and only once every provider has answered, New chat says so and uses the computer's default; Settings shows the same note. Forgetting a computer drops its phone defaults. A share into Nekko (Share to Nekko) uses the same defaults.
- **Share to Nekko** `[implemented 2026-10-14; verified on an API 37 emulator against a sandbox agent (share sheet entry, lock first, editable text on New chat, text in the new chat's composer and not sent, Back/remove/blank/cold start/rotation); not yet on a physical device]`: user intent is "send text or a link from another app to open a new chat". Nekko Agent appears in Android's share sheet for `text/plain` (`ACTION_SEND`); browsers' page title (subject) is kept above the link unless the text already contains it. Choosing it opens **New chat** on the active computer with a *Shared with Nekko* card: the text is editable there and can be removed. After *Start on …* the text is in the new chat's message box and the user presses Send; **Nekko never sends shared text by itself**. The app lock applies first (a share while locked shows the lock, then New chat). Shared text is held in memory only (never written to disk, gone if the process dies before the chat starts), Back or removing it drops it, a newer share replaces an unused one. Limits: text only (files and images are not accepted, so no URI read permission is involved; attachments are AN-8), cut at 20,000 characters with a visible note, control characters removed; the computer and model come from the existing New chat defaults (A5 will add phone-side defaults).
- **Vibration on agent events** `[implemented 2026-10-15; verified on an API 37 emulator against a sandbox agent through the system vibrator log (approval pattern, preview, finished on/off, silent, Do Not Disturb, background); not felt on a physical device]`: user intent is "vibrate on events, for example when the agent asks something". *Settings → Vibration* has one switch per `PhoneEvent`: **Needs your approval** (on by default, two firm pulses), **Asks you a question** (on, one long pulse), **Reply finished** (off, one short tap), **Reply failed** (off, three short taps); switching one on plays its pattern once. It vibrates only while the app is in the foreground (`ProcessLifecycleOwner` STARTED) for the active computer's agent events, never in silent mode or Do Not Disturb, and with notification usage so the system's own vibration setting also applies. In the background the planned push notifications own sound and vibration (AN-6), so an event never buzzes twice. A sub-agent's chat finishing or failing does not buzz; its approvals and questions do. At most one vibration per 1.5 s. Adds the install-time `VIBRATE` permission (no prompt). Stored on the phone in `PhonePrefs` (`haptics.*`).
- **The computer's settings from the phone** `[implemented 2026-10-15; verified on an API 37 emulator against a sandbox agent running the #529 phone policy (each change read back from the computer's settings.json, provider keys untouched; complete and delete on the computer's session files; offline state); not yet on a physical device or against the desktop daemon]`: user intent is "set the PC's default chat mode, the chat action and delete confirmation from the phone", with YOLO allowed. *Settings → On <computer>* shows and changes three of the computer's own settings (the same ones as in its Settings, not copies): **Chat mode for new chats** (Ask / Guardrails / YOLO), **Long-press a chat to** (Complete, the desktop's archive, or Delete), and **Confirm before deleting**. Each change is one `settings:update` with only that key; the screen shows the computer's reply, so it reflects what was saved there. **YOLO is allowed from a phone** by the owner's decision, already enforced on the computer since #529 (`PHONE_CHAT_MODES` in packages/shared/src/phone-access.ts and `chat_modes` in crates/nekko-remote/src/phone.rs, both tested); the phone asks once ("Use YOLO for new chats?") before switching to it. Nothing is shown until the computer has answered, so its values are never guessed (offline: a line saying so). In the chat list, a long-press offers the computer's action for that chat: Complete sets `archivedAt` (`session:setOptions`, nothing is deleted), Delete calls `session:delete`, after a "Delete this chat?" confirmation unless the computer is set not to ask. A protocol test checks every channel, setting key and chat mode the phone uses against the computer's phone policy file, so a phone feature the computer would refuse fails the build.
- **Dictation** `[implemented 2026-10-15; verified on an API 37 emulator against a sandbox agent with real Whisper (whisper.cpp via Homebrew, the managed Tiny.en Q5_1 model) by injecting recorded speech into the emulator microphone: mic → WAV → sealed relay → Whisper → text in the message box, not sent; silence, permission refused, the computer's voice off; the phone recognizer path only to its start because the emulator crashes (SIGSEGV) when audio is injected while Google's recognizer listens; not yet on a physical device]`: user intent is "send audio directly from the phone", explicitly "more fun than useful but worth doing", and the desktop already has voice. A microphone button in the chat's message box (while the box is empty or not) records and turns speech into text that is **inserted into the message box, never sent**: the user reads and presses Send. Two engines, chosen in *Settings → Dictation* (phone-side, `PhonePrefs` `dictation.*`): **<computer> when ready** (default) uses the computer's Nekko Voice (Whisper) through the end-to-end encrypted relay (`voice:transcribe`, already allowed to phones by #529; nothing changes on the computer), falling back to the phone when the computer can't (offline, Nekko Voice not installed or switched off there); **<computer> only** never falls back; **This phone** uses Android's `SpeechRecognizer` with on-device preferred, and Settings says the audio may go to Google depending on the phone. Computer recordings are 16 kHz mono 16-bit PCM WAV, in memory only, **capped at 30 s** with a visible clock and an automatic stop: the computer accepts 60 s, but the WAV travels as a JSON number array, sealed and base64'd (~150 KB/s), so 60 s (~9.1 MB) would sit at the relay's 10 MB frame limit; 30 s is ~4.6 MB. Recordings under 0.25 s are not sent; Whisper's non-speech tags (`[BLANK_AUDIO]`, `[Music]`, `(wind blowing)`) are dropped, and nothing heard says so instead of inserting them. The microphone permission is asked on the first tap; refusing it explains where to allow it. The phone's dictation setting is separate from the computer's `voice.enabled` (which means "Whisper installed and on there"); the phone only reads that, to decide and to say why. The computer uses the language set in its own Nekko Voice settings; the phone recognizer's language is set on the phone.
- **Same security boundary as the Expo app:** every payload is E2E-sealed (PBKDF2-SHA256 100k → AES-256-GCM, salt `nekko-relay:<room>`), the relay sees only ciphertext, and the HELLO handshake is enforced by the computer's device registry (unknown devices denied, revoked devices kicked, spent codes refused). Pairing material (room, secret, derived key, device id) is encrypted at rest under a non-exportable Android Keystore AES-GCM key and excluded from backups and device transfer. Provider records and settings are reduced to ids, labels and the default model the moment they arrive: API keys never reach app state, storage or logs. Release builds allow TLS (`wss://`) only; debug builds additionally allow `ws://` to localhost / the emulator host for a local test relay.

- **Background connection and local notifications** `[implemented 2026-10-14; verified on an API 37 emulator against a local relay and headless agent: reconnect after airplane mode, keep-alive service while a run streams with the app in the background, finished/failed notifications with and without the app lock, opening a chat through the lock; not verified on Android 9–13, physical devices, OEM battery managers, Wi-Fi↔cellular handover on real radios, or approval/question notifications live]`: user intent is "the phone stays in touch with a running chat when I put it in my pocket, and tells me when it's done or needs me". **Reconnect:** a `ConnectivityManager` default-network callback (`connection/NetworkChanges.kt`, JVM-tested) restarts the relay socket as soon as the phone moves to another network or a network comes back after none; `RelayClient.reconnect()` drops the old socket, fails in-flight calls and dials at once (no-op once closed or denied); the backoff otherwise stays as it was. Measured on the emulator with 40 s of airplane mode: back online after 21.5 s and 26.6 s before, 4.4–4.7 s after (see docs/perf-history.md). **Keep-alive:** *Settings → Connection → Stay connected while a chat runs* (`PhonePrefs` `keep_connected`, on by default) runs `KeepAliveService`, a foreground service of type `remoteMessaging` (no runtime permission and no daily cap, unlike `dataSync`, which Android 15 limits to 6 h a day), only while a chat on the active computer is running or waiting on the user, the relay is online or reconnecting, and an event arrived in the last 30 minutes (`connection/KeepAlive.kt`, JVM-tested). It is started when the app leaves the foreground (the moment Android allows it) and stops itself when the run ends; its notification is silent, low-importance and reads "Staying connected · N chat(s) running on <computer>". It holds no connection of its own and is not sticky. `REQUEST_IGNORE_BATTERY_OPTIMIZATIONS` is deliberately not used (Play restricts it); when the app is *Restricted* in battery settings, Settings warns and opens the app's system page. **Notifications:** *Notify me* (`notify_events`, on by default; POST_NOTIFICATIONS is requested from Settings on Android 13+, never at launch) posts a local notification for `PhoneEvent`s (reply ready, reply failed, question waiting, approval needed) only while the app is out of sight and only for chats the phone lists. Two channels, *Approvals and questions* (high) and *Replies* (default), plus *Connection* for the keep-alive. Text never carries the reply, command or question: only the event, the chat title and the computer; with the app lock on, not even the title ("Open Nekko to see it."), and visibility is PRIVATE on the lock screen. One notification per chat (tag `chat:<sessionId>`), replaced by newer events and cleared when the chat is opened; push (AN-6) must reuse the same tag so a push and a local notice never show twice. Tapping opens the chat through `nekko-agent://chat/<id>`, so it passes the app lock. Without push these only arrive while Android keeps the app alive. Found on the way: both loops report a stop between steps as `error "Aborted"` (not only `"Stopped"`), so `PhoneEvent` now treats both as a user stop, not a failure.

- **Settings layout** `[implemented 2026-10-14; checked on a headless emulator, light and dark]`: the phone's Settings screen is an ordered list of sections, one file each in `ui/settings/` (Appearance, Security, Computers, About), so later phone settings each add a section instead of editing one screen. *Computers* opens the existing paired-computers screen; *About* shows the installed version (read from the package) and the open-source licenses: the app's MIT license and the Apache 2.0 text bundled with the app, and the Google Play services / ML Kit terms for the code scanner, which is not open source. The list of libraries is kept by hand from the release runtime classpath (no license-plugin dependency). No new functional setting is in this change. Groundwork for later settings, not visible yet: `PhonePrefs`, a store for the phone's non-secret settings (a `StateFlow` per key, its own preferences file `nekko_phone`, excluded from backup and device transfer like everything else in the app; the appearance choice stays where it was, in `ThemePrefs`), and `PhoneEvent` (finished, failed, needs input, approval), a pure mapping from the agent events the chat already receives, not yet wired to any notification or vibration. A stopped turn (`error` "Stopped") is not a failure.

**Not in this version `[planned]`:** models running on the phone (the Expo app's llama.rn feature; on Android this needs a llama.cpp JNI/NDK bridge), push notifications (FCM token registration to the relay), Markdown rendering (replies show as plain text; a long press copies a whole message, there is no partial text selection so that a press-and-drag always scrolls the chat), attachments, and Play Store distribution (signing key, listing, Data safety form).

**Host fix found by this work `[implemented, tested]`:** the relay agent sealed each outgoing frame independently with async WebCrypto, so a streamed reply could reach a phone out of order ("from your Hello computer."). Outgoing sealed frames now go through one ordered queue in `packages/host/src/relay.ts` (covered by `relay.test.ts`); this affects every relay client, including the Expo app and the web edition.

**Verification so far:** 71 JVM tests (protocol, relay state machine against a fake agent, transcript reconciliation, chat logic, repository, ask form); the Kotlin crypto produces byte-identical output to WebCrypto and the agent's `packages/shared` implementation opens Kotlin-sealed frames; a live integration test drives the real relay and a headless agent with a fake model (enrollment, provider-key stripping, streamed turn, a guarded `rm -rf` held for approval and denied from the phone, reconnection without a code, refusal of a stranger and of a spent code); lint is clean and debug + minified release APKs build. **Unverified:** the Compose UI on an emulator or device (layout, themes, keyboard, deep links, scanner, Keystore on real hardware), pairing against the managed relay, and the TalkBack/large-font pass.

### Wall new-chat details placement

New-chat details belong **inside the same bordered Add to the wall box**, never in a full-width section above existing windows. Grid/Fixed retain the tile position and width while it grows vertically as needed; Focus exposes an equivalent tile below the hero when opened from the toolbar. Details, model choice, errors and existing sessions stay contained at narrow/wide widths and in both themes. Opening/closing must not change existing window coordinates or saved layout. Close/Escape and creation/retry retain existing behavior. Implementation and evidence status are recorded in TASKS.md.

### Your agent in your pocket (the iOS and Android app)

`[in build 2026-10-03]` A native app for iPhone and Android with three tabs: **Chats**, **On this phone**, and **Computers**. It is built for one-handed check-ins rather than as a copy of the desktop.

- **First run.** Nekko and two choices: *Pair your computer* (scan the QR from Settings → Remote access, or paste the link) or *Chat on this phone* (download a model). Notification permission is asked only after a computer is paired, never at first launch.
- **Chats** lists the computer's chats (newest first, with the last reply as a preview) and the phone's own chats in a second section. A chat that is mid-run shows **Working**; one waiting on an approval or a question shows **Needs you**, including approvals that were already waiting when the phone connected. A line at the top says whether the computer is online, offline (asleep, app closed) or reconnecting, and turns into "pair again" if the computer removed this phone. Pull to refresh.
- **Starting a chat** asks where it should run. On the computer: its default model is preselected, any of its providers' models can be picked, and the chat can be pointed at one of its project folders. On the phone: any downloaded model.
- **A computer chat** shows the conversation as the desktop does, compactly: replies render Markdown, the model's reasoning folds away, each tool step is one line (its file, command or query, with a status mark) that expands to its output. Replies stream as they are written. When the agent wants to do something risky, an **Allow this?** card shows the exact command and the risk level, with Approve and Deny, and the phone buzzes. When the agent asks a question, its options appear as tappable choices with a free-text answer and Skip. While a run is going, a new message queues as a follow-up, exactly as on the desktop; the send button becomes Stop. Reconnecting after the phone slept re-reads the chat, so nothing streamed in the meantime is lost.
- **On this phone** is a short, curated list of models that run on the phone itself through llama.cpp (Metal on iPhone, OpenCL or CPU on Android): Qwen3.5 0.8B, 2B and 4B, Gemma 4 E2B and E4B, and Llama 3.2 1B. Each shows its exact download size, license, and how well it fits this phone's memory (*Runs great*, *Runs well*, *Tight fit*, or *Too big*, which cannot be downloaded); one is marked **Recommended** for the phone's memory, the everyday 2B where it fits comfortably and the tiny one otherwise. Downloads come from Hugging Face with live progress and can be cancelled; a download that arrives short is discarded rather than loaded. Downloaded models can be made the default or deleted.
- **A phone chat** runs entirely on the device with no network and nothing leaving the phone. The model loads on first use (with progress), replies stream, generation speed shows under the last reply, and models that can reason get a **Think** switch (off by default, for speed). One model is in memory at a time. Phone chats are saved on the phone only.
- **Computers** lists paired computers with their connection state, relay and pairing age; the first connection names a computer after its OS ("Windows PC", "Mac"), and it can be renamed, switched to, reconnected, or forgotten (which deletes the key from the phone and reminds the user to remove the phone on the computer too). Several computers can be paired; the app talks to one at a time.
- **Security** is the remote-control model above, unchanged: pairing keys and this phone's identity live in the iOS Keychain / Android Keystore, the encryption key is derived once on pairing and cached there, the one-time code is never resent after the first welcome, and provider API keys the computer returns are dropped on arrival and never stored on the phone.

Where remote control is the whole app on a phone, **messaging channels** are the lighter touch: messaging the agent the way you message a person, from an app you already have open. Configured under **Connectors → Messaging channels**, a channel long-polls its provider (no public URL, no inbound ports) and each allowed chat binds to one Nekko Agent session, so a reply in that conversation continues it rather than starting over. The agent's answer streams into a single message that keeps editing itself instead of one message per delta; approvals and the agent's questions arrive as inline buttons and resolve with a tap, and a turn started in the desktop app on a bound session mirrors its reply into the channel. Trust is explicit: channels are off by default, and only the listed chat ids can reach the agent, everything else is refused before a session exists. Telegram is the first adapter; Slack and WhatsApp Business follow the same seam.

## What Success Looks Like

- **Offline PC control target (planned):** on a supported machine, a non-expert can review and approve one setup plan, complete required OS permission prompts, and use the selected intent/speech stack for approved local actions with external network access blocked. Model choices meet verified whole-stack minimum requirements, sensitive actions require confirmation, and stop/revocation reliably prevents subsequent actions. Unsupported hardware or capabilities are explained rather than hidden by cloud fallback.

- **"Connect + use a local model" just works**: one-click local setup, and the model can actually read/edit/run in a real codebase end-to-end (verified repeatedly against a live LM Studio gemma reasoning model: streaming reasoning, single + multi-step tool loops, index-grounded context).
- **The unique features land**: users can *see* what the model is given (Context Inspector) and trust it on risky commands (guardrails), the two differentiators from the original ask.
- **Same app everywhere**: desktop, one-command web, Docker, and (paid) hosted, with a byte-identical UI.
- **Polished enough to stand next to Zed/Cursor/Warp**: whitespace, themes, speed, and a charming mascot.
- **Shipped and installable**: public MIT repo with downloadable installers for win/mac/linux, auto-updates, and a marketing site with downloads. macOS builds are signed with a Nekko Labs Developer ID certificate and notarized by Apple, so a download installs with no Gatekeeper workaround; Windows signing (SignPath Foundation) is still pending.

## Feature Set

> Living catalog of capabilities, grouped by area, marked `[shipped]` / `[in progress]` / `[planned]`. Capability-level descriptions; the task-level breakdown (with stable IDs and history) lives in [TASKS.md](TASKS.md) Part 2, and the full technical design for the web/Docker/Cloud editions lives in [spec-web-and-hosted.md](spec-web-and-hosted.md).

### Speed & responsiveness (the speed contract)

Agent tools feel slow when the UI waits on something or redraws too much. Nekko Agent treats responsiveness as a feature with numbers, and CI fails a change that breaks them. The desktop UI stays web technology (Electron + React) for maintainability; the engine behind it moves into a separate Rust daemon so the window never shares a process with the agent loop, the terminals or the model server. `[in progress]` *As of 2026-09-30 (PF1-PF7): the chat composer, streaming, windowed-transcript, incremental-markdown and instant-switching rules hold, the engine runs in its own daemon with a terminal that keeps up, and every budget, the terminal's included, is measured in CI. The web, npx and Docker editions still run the TS host's terminals until they move behind the daemon (PF14).*

**The rules.**

- **The UI thread never does disk or network work.** Every read and write goes to the engine asynchronously; nothing on the input path waits on a file, a socket or the engine.
- **A keypress shows on screen within one frame: 8 ms at 120 Hz.** This holds in the chat composer and in the terminal, in a chat with a thousand messages, while a reply is streaming. It is measured in CI on every change.
- **Only the part of the transcript that is on screen is rendered.** A chat with thousands of messages scrolls and types as fast as a chat with ten.
- **Streaming markdown is parsed incrementally.** Finished blocks of a reply are parsed once and left alone; only the block still being written is re-parsed, at most once per frame.
- **Decorations do not cost a frame each.** `[implemented 2026-10-12]` The looping decorations (a working agent's beam and status rocket, the pixel cat, the needs-you glow, pulsing dots) animate things the compositor cannot run alone, so Chromium used to tick them on the main thread on every frame, and the page never went idle while an agent worked. Their steps are 15 fps or slower, so the app now pauses them and advances them on a 30 Hz clock of its own: the same frames at the same moments to within a tick, an idle main thread in between, and still held while the window is hidden or unfocused.
- **Local state updates first; the engine catches up.** Sending, renaming, pinning, switching and closing take effect on screen immediately. If the engine later refuses, the change is rolled back and the reason is shown.

**Switching is instant, history may follow.** Switching chats, windows, workspaces or tabs changes the screen in the next frame. The chats used most recently stay warm: their transcripts are kept in a bounded in-memory cache and the last few chat windows stay mounted but hidden, so returning to one is a visibility flip rather than a rebuild. A chat that is not warm shows its frame (title, composer, controls) in the next frame and its history a moment later, newest messages first. The warm set has a fixed ceiling in both chats and memory, so the app does not grow without bound the longer it runs. `[implemented 2026-10-12]` On the Agents wall in Focus, the windows that are not shown stay laid out under the one that is, at its size, but are not drawn (`content-visibility: hidden`), so switching back reuses their styles and layout; the last four Focus chats also keep their composer mounted while hidden. Selecting a window restyles only its frame, never its transcript. Measured on the speed contract (CI runner), a warm switch went from a p95 of 24-30 ms to 13-17 ms.

**Budgets** (p95, headless, against the web edition with a scripted model). The target is the product budget, measured on a desktop reference machine at 120 Hz, and `npm run perf:strict` holds a run to it. CI runs the same measurements on a 4-vCPU GitHub runner with no GPU and a 60 Hz display, where the same work takes about twice as long and varies about 30% run to run, so the `perf` job is a regression gate at two and a half times the target (three times for the composer keypress and the cold-switch frame, which the runner had at 100-110% of the 2.5x gate on every attempt by 2026-10-03 while a desktop measured them at 8 and 12 ms). The report prints the raw p95 next to both numbers. `[updated 2026-10-12]` The latency figures measure the app, not the headless display's frame clock: the browser they run in has its frame-rate limiter off, and the harness keeps its frames flowing with a 1 px compositor animation of its own, because Chromium otherwise holds the frame after one that drew nothing to a 60 Hz tick. Before that, a keypress or switch measured up to a frame slower whenever nothing on the page happened to be animating (a chat switch read 18-19 ms instead of 10-13 ms on the runner with the app doing the same work). The report prints this frame wake next to the budgets.

| Interaction | Target (reference desktop, 120 Hz) | CI regression gate (2.5x, or 3x where marked) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Keypress to paint, chat composer, 1,000-message chat, reply streaming | 8.3 ms | 25 ms (3x) |
| Keypress to paint, terminal | 8.3 ms | 20.8 ms |
| Main-thread work per frame while a reply streams at 300 tokens/s | 4 ms | 10 ms |
| Switch to a warm chat, to paint | 8.3 ms | 20.8 ms |
| Switch to a cold chat, frame painted | 8.3 ms | 25 ms (3x) |
| Switch to a cold chat, newest screenful of history painted | 100 ms | 250 ms |

**The memory contract.** `[added 2026-10-10]` A long session must not slow the app, or the rest of the desktop, down. A wall of busy agents once held ~70 MB of GPU tiles per window for a decorative beam, filled Chromium's 512 MB tile budget ("tile memory limits exceeded") and starved Windows' own compositor. So:

- **No animated layer is bigger than what it shows.** A decoration that runs an animation or carries `will-change` is rastered whole, so it is drawn at the size of its own box, never as an oversized surface clipped down to a ring or a glow.
- **Nothing a chat switch builds is kept.** Switching round the chats again and again leaves the heap, the DOM and the event listeners where the first lap left them.
- **Helpers end what they start.** Every browser, server or child process the app or its tooling spawns is ended with its whole process tree, and its scratch profile deleted. The tooling uses Chromium (Playwright's or Chrome), never the user's own Edge.

The same `perf` job measures these, on a Grid wall of six agents streaming at 2x scale, with a software GPU (SwiftShader) so a CI runner and a desktop count tiles the same way (`node scripts/perf/run.mjs --only memory`; budgets in `scripts/perf/budgets.mjs`):

| Check | CI gate |
| --- | --- |
| Worst animated compositor layer against the box that clips it | 2x |
| Compositor tile memory | 320 MB |
| JS heap kept per lap of chat switches, after warm-up | 2 MB |
| DOM nodes kept per lap | 50 |
| Event listeners kept per lap | 20 |
