**The engine can fail without taking the window with it.** `[shipped 2026-09-30, desktop]` The desktop window no longer runs the engine: a separate engine daemon (`nekkod`, in Rust) owns the terminals and supervises the rest of the engine as its own process. If either crashes it is restarted and the UI reconnects; the window, drafts and scroll positions survive. If the daemon binary is missing, the app still starts, running the rest of the engine directly with its older terminals.

**The terminal keeps up with any output.** `[shipped 2026-09-30, desktop]` Terminals render on the GPU with xterm.js on WebGL by default. Ghostty's terminal core compiled to WebAssembly is offered in Settings as an experimental renderer: it draws correctly, but under a 40 MB flood it held the UI thread 20-30 ms a frame where xterm stayed inside one, so it is not the default until that changes. Output is delivered at most once per frame rather than once per chunk, and a flood of output (a large `cat`, a runaway build log) slows the program producing it instead of freezing the app. Measured on the desktop app: a keypress in another input while a visible terminal prints 40 MB reaches the screen in 7.1 ms p95 (7.6 ms before), and the longest frame dropped from 20.9 ms to 4.4 ms. Keystroke echo through the engine takes under 1 ms on its own; PowerShell adds about 15 ms of its own before it echoes, which no terminal can remove (Command Prompt and Git Bash echo in under 1 ms). Prompt themes built on Nerd Fonts render when one is installed.

### How it's built: the window, the engine daemon and the TS host

*A plain-language overview of the moving parts, for anyone reading the code or the task list. "TS" means TypeScript.* `[in progress]` *As of 2026-10-01.*

**The window's first size** `[updated 2026-10-03]`: on a first launch (nothing saved yet) the desktop window opens at **70% of the screen's width and 80% of its height**, centred on the work area and never below 900 × 600; the size is decided before the window is created and the window is shown only once its page is ready, so it appears at that size rather than growing into it on screen. From then on the size and position the user leaves it at are what it reopens with.

On the desktop, Nekko Agent runs as three processes:

| Part | Written in | What it does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **The window** | TypeScript (React in Electron) | Everything you see and type into. It never does disk, network or agent work itself; it asks the engine. |
| **The engine daemon, `nekkod`** | Rust | The engine's front door. The window talks only to it, over one local connection. It answers what has already been rebuilt in Rust and passes every other request on to the TS host unchanged. It starts the TS host, restarts it if it crashes, and outlives it. |
| **The TS host** | TypeScript (on Node) | The original engine (`packages/host`). Everything not moved yet still runs here. |

**Why split it.** The engine used to be one TypeScript process doing everything at once, so a long reply, a big file search or a slow tool in one chat could hold up opening another chat or typing in a terminal. Rebuilding the engine in Rust, piece by piece, gives each job its own lane, keeps loaded models and running servers alive when the rest of the engine restarts, and takes the heaviest work off the TypeScript event loop. The window itself stays web technology on purpose (see "Native desktop shell" above).

**How a piece moves.** One service at a time. Each request the window can make has a name; once the daemon handles a name itself it answers in Rust, and every other name still goes to the TS host, so the window never changes when something moves. Each Rust version is held to the TypeScript original by recorded test data (golden files): the TypeScript code writes down what it does on a set of cases, and the Rust code must reproduce it exactly. Each move also gets a live check on a real local model before it merges. Where the Rust version deliberately behaves differently (stopping a whole process tree rather than one process, say), the task list says so.

**Already in the daemon:**

- Terminals (2026-09-30).
- The local model servers (llama.cpp, stable-diffusion.cpp) and the OpenAI-compatible address in front of them, so loaded models survive engine restarts (2026-09-30).
- Decision models (Laya) (2026-10-01).
- Reading and saving chats: the chat list, opening a chat, and the user's own edits (rename, pin, options, queued follow-ups). With the TS host busy, opening a 7 MB chat went from 403 ms to 14 ms and listing 301 chats from 797 ms to 22 ms (2026-10-01).
- The agent loop that produces a chat reply, streaming from the model providers, with the reply's tokens going straight from the daemon to the window (2026-10-01).
- The short side calls after a reply: the chat's title, suggested replies, and filling in part of a prompt (2026-10-01).
- MCP servers (2026-10-01).
- The built-in file and shell tools (read, write, edit, list, glob, grep, bash), their guardrails, and the list of pending file changes (2026-10-01).
- The whole chat turn (2026-10-12): the tools on offer, the system prompt, the context read from disk (guidelines, memory, attachments), saving the user's message and every step, the agent's questions, setting the chat's title, completing it, an ordinary chat's plan, and Claude subscription turns (their rate-limit headers are passed to the TS host). Anything waiting on a person, tool approvals and the agent's questions, is held and answered in the daemon, so the board, the chat and the phone all answer it there; with the TS host busy, approving a tool went from 451 ms to 6 ms.

**Still in the TS host:**

- Admitting each turn: checking the provider and chat, setting up a chat's worktree, syncing MCP servers, a fresh sign-in token, and the connector and workspace-search snippets. The daemon then runs the turn, so a turn's first text still waits on a busy TS host.
- The tools not rebuilt yet: sub-agents, MCP calls, `decide`, the browser, processes, web fetch, agent watches, training reports. The daemon calls the TS host for them; their approvals are held in the daemon too.
- After a turn: recording usage and the reply log, tool hooks, and admitting the next queued message (the daemon then runs it, and writes the chat's generated title itself).
- Settings, connectors, messaging, workflows, training runs, Hypergate discovery and model downloads.

**The phone relay in the daemon.** `[implemented 2026-10-12]` Remote access is the daemon's when it knows the data directory (the desktop app, and the web, npx and Docker editions behind it): `remote.json`, the device registry, pairing codes, and the relay agent that dials out to the relay. The protocol is unchanged for every phone app and for `apps/relay` (relay v2, the sealed HELLO, PBKDF2 and AES-GCM framing, the push ping when a run finishes), held to the TS code byte for byte. A paired phone now reaches exactly what the UI reaches, through the daemon's own router, so its requests land on the daemon's services rather than on stale copies in the TS host, and it hears the same events the UI hears. What it may call is decided on the computer, not by the phone (see "What a paired phone may do" below); the daemon's internal callbacks (`turn:*`, `loop:*` and the like), which the TS relay's dispatcher happened to expose, stay refused. Frames go out in the order they were produced. The TS agent stays only for the headless relay-agent mode (`NEKKO_RELAY_URL`) and the editions without a daemon. Not yet verified: the native iOS and Android apps against it (both use the same protocol, which the golden set pins), and the managed relay (never used for testing with real devices).

**Session clearing.** The daemon now implements clear today, this month and all when configured with a data directory. Date windows use inclusive local calendar boundaries against the chat's last update, not its creation date; archived and pinned chats are not exempt. Clear and single-session delete coordinate command-log removal through the host callback before deleting chat files, preserving the host's queued removal and process-lifetime append tombstone. Callback failures stop deletion rather than bypassing the queue. TS-generated parity fixtures verify deletion counts, retained files and summary-cache refresh; focused host tests verify queued-write removal and later append suppression, and daemon HTTP tests verify callback IDs. Month cutoffs resolve today's midnight before changing the date to day 1, matching TS setter order. Deterministic JS/Rust timezone fixtures verify midnight gaps, repeated midnight, skipped calendar days and month setter order. An isolated real-daemon/real-host-dispatcher HTTP harness verifies queued writes and racing appends cannot recreate removed logs. The host callback awaits queued unlink and propagates failure; daemon tests verify failed callbacks and disabled backends preserve JSON for both delete and clear. Deletion requires the host backend to be available because it owns the log queue and tombstone; unavailable backends fail closed. Non-Windows execution remains unverified. Without a daemon data directory, the TS host remains the fallback. This does not complete PF14.

**Automation tasks in the daemon.** `[implemented 2026-10-12]` Scheduled, recurring and background tasks are the daemon's when it knows the data directory: `tasks.json`, the task channels and the scheduler. A task still fires through a normal chat turn (the host admits it, as for any chat), keeps its own chat across fires, and the file format is unchanged. Two behaviors changed: a task now fires when it is due (the scheduler sleeps until the earliest due task) instead of up to 15 seconds late, and a task whose fire was cut short by a restart fires again at startup instead of never firing again. Without a daemon, the TS host runs tasks as before, with the same restart fix.

**Workflows in the daemon.** `[implemented 2026-10-12]` The workflow engine runs in the daemon when it knows the data directory: definitions, run history, connector-poll cursors, the schedule, connector listeners and webhooks. Prompt and skill steps are still normal chat turns, actions and shell steps behave as before (a timed-out shell step now stops everything it started), and the files keep their format. Without a daemon the TS host runs workflows as before.

**What a paired phone may do.** `[implemented 2026-10-14, both relay agents; not yet verified against the native apps on a device or the managed relay]` User intent: the limit on what a phone can do is enforced by the computer; locking the phone app is not a boundary. One allowlist in `packages/shared/src/phone-access.ts` is the source of truth; the TS relay agent (`packages/host/src/relay.ts`) applies it directly and the daemon's (`crates/nekko-remote/src/phone.rs`) reads the same lists from `crates/nekko-remote/src/phone-access.json`, which the TS golden test writes and fails on when stale; the same test records how the TS decides a set of requests (`tests/golden/phone.json`) and the Rust gate must reproduce every refusal message, host call and reply. Acceptance criteria:
- A phone may call only: `app:info`, `chat:abort`, `chat:answer`, `chat:pending`, `chat:queue`, `chat:send`, `models:list`, `providers:list`, `session:create`, `session:delete`, `session:get`, `session:setOptions`, `sessions:summaries`, `settings:get`, `settings:update` (filtered), `tool:approve`, `voice:status`, `voice:transcribe`, `workspace:list`: what the Expo, Android and iOS clients use, plus settings, completing/deleting a chat as the desktop does (`session:setOptions` with `archivedAt`, `session:delete`) and voice, for the next phone releases. Anything else is refused before the host runs it, with `Not allowed from a paired phone (<code>): <detail>`.
- Always refused even if added to the list: `data:wipe`, `settings:reset`, `file:write`, `workspace:addByPath`, `workspace:remove`, `voice:install`, `voice:uninstall`, `voice:cancel`, `connector:connect`, `connector:disconnect`, `providers:save`, `providers:remove`, `providers:importCliAuth`, and every `remote:*` (except `remote:status`, itself not on the list), `terminal:*`, `terminals:*`, `mcp:*` and `oauth:*` channel. Hooks are settings, so they are covered by the settings rule.
- `settings:update` from a phone may set only `defaultChatMode`, `chatPaneAction`, `skipDeleteConfirmation`, `voice.enabled` and `voice.language`, each with a checked value; any other key refuses the whole update and nothing is applied. A voice change keeps the rest of the saved `voice` object.
- **YOLO from a phone `[decided 2026-10-14]`:** the owner decided a paired phone may choose YOLO, both as `defaultChatMode` and as one chat's `mode`, with no confirmation on the computer. The chat modes a phone may pick are `PHONE_CHAT_MODES` (`ask`, `guardrails`, `yolo`); any other value is `value-invalid`.
- `settings:get` (and the reply to `settings:update`) returns an allowlisted view: theme, theme preset, accents, default provider/model, starred models, and the phone-settable settings; no provider records, keys, tokens, folders, hooks, guardrails or instructions. `providers:list` returns id, kind, label and enabled only.
- `session:setOptions` from a phone may set the model, title, pin, tags, `archivedAt`, thinking and the chat mode (YOLO included); not tools, execution mode, Git isolation, offline or incognito. `chat:send` from a phone may not attach paths on the computer.
- Consequence: the web app opened through a pairing link over the relay (`apps/desktop/src/renderer/web-client.ts`) is held to the same list, so most desktop screens (training, goals, Command Center, terminals, settings) no longer work there. This replaces the earlier "a phone browser gets the full app" promise in the remote-control section.

**Editions.** `[updated 2026-10-12]` The web edition (`npm run web`, the in-repo server the CI speed contract measures) now runs behind the daemon too, in the desktop's topology: the server starts `nekkod` on a random loopback port with a per-launch token, `nekkod` starts the TS host as its backend (this same server script under Node, since there is no Electron), and the server stays the public front door. It keeps every existing rule for who may connect (localhost by default; `NEKKO_TOKEN`, `NEKKO_ALLOW_UNAUTHENTICATED`, `NEKKO_ALLOWED_HOSTS` and `NEKKO_ALLOWED_ORIGINS` for remote and Docker), applies the same Host, Origin and token checks to sockets, and passes requests to the daemon with the daemon's own token, so the public token never reaches the daemon, its backend or a terminal's environment, and no URL carrying a token is logged. Terminals stream over the daemon's binary `/api/term/:id` socket, as on the desktop. If either process crashes it is restarted with backoff (the server restarts `nekkod`, `nekkod` restarts the backend), and stopping the server, or killing it outright, leaves no process behind. Without a daemon binary the server says so in one log line and runs the engine in its own process as before; `NEKKO_DAEMON=nekkod` makes a missing or failing daemon an error instead, `NEKKO_DAEMON=ts` forces the in-process engine, and `NEKKOD_PATH` names a binary. The server looks for `NEKKOD_PATH`, then the platform's npm package, then `bin/` beside the script, then a cargo build in the checkout. The npx package (`npx nekko-agent-web`) installs this platform's daemon from an optional dependency, `@nekko-agent/nekkod-<platform>-<arch>` (Windows x64, Linux x64 and arm64, macOS arm64; ONNX Runtime has no prebuilt library for Intel macOS, which keeps the in-process engine), built and published by the release workflow at the same version; a platform without one, or a binary that will not start (the Linux builds need a GCC 13 C++ runtime: Ubuntu 24.04, Debian 13 or newer), falls back to the in-process engine with the log line. The Docker image builds the daemon in its own stage and runs the web edition behind it (`NEKKO_DAEMON=nekkod` is set, so a broken daemon stops the container rather than quietly falling back), on Debian 13 with `tini` as PID 1 so `docker stop` shuts everything down cleanly, and a health check on `/health`; `docker compose` is unchanged for users. Not yet: the relay-agent mode (`NEKKO_RELAY_URL`) still runs the engine in-process.

**Falling back.** If the daemon binary is missing, the desktop app still starts and runs the TS host directly. For diagnosing a problem, setting `NEKKO_AGENT_LOOP=ts` keeps whole turns, side calls, MCP servers and the built-in tools in the TS host; chats and pending changes stay in the daemon. Sandbox chats always run their turns in the TS host, with its container router.

**The end state.** Everything moves, the TS host is retired, and the web, npx and Docker editions ship the daemon too. Remaining in order: the rest of a turn (admitting it), then connectors, messaging and workflows, and training (the phone relay's agent moved on 2026-10-12). The plan and each step's details are in `TASKS.md` (PF14).

### Models & providers

- **Provider support** `[shipped]`, Anthropic (native), OpenAI-compatible streaming SSE (covers OpenAI/OpenRouter/LM Studio/vLLM), Ollama native (list/pull/ps/load/unload), with OpenRouter presets. Base-URL `/v1` normalization for bare `host:port` entries. `[updated 2026-09-30]` OpenRouter is now a first-class provider rather than a preset: one-click **OAuth sign-in** (PKCE, browser authorize, loopback callback, the returned API key stored host-side and never shown to the renderer) alongside the plain API-key path; its `/key` endpoint validates a credential and reports usage, its model list carries real per-token pricing, its requests identify the app (`HTTP-Referer`, `X-Title`), and its errors (rate limits, exhausted credits, bad key) surface as readable text instead of a raw status. `[updated 2026-10-09]` Anthropic's model list is **live, not compiled in**: `GET /v1/models` lists every model the credential can call, API key or subscription sign-in, under the API's own names and context windows, so a new Claude model appears without an app release. The picker groups it by family, Opus, Sonnet, Fable, Mythos, then Haiku, newest first within each. The built-in list (`[updated 2026-10-10]` now including Haiku 5.5) is the fallback when the call fails, returns nothing, takes longer than five seconds, or no credential is set; the app's model table fills a window the API leaves out.
- **Local-model auto-discovery** `[shipped]`, scans localhost + `127.0.0.1` on common ports; Test-connection in the add form; cards auto-check and show Connected/Offline; Local vs Cloud grouping. `[updated 2026-10-13]` Only **enabled** cards auto-check; see "Provider on/off switch".
- **Provider on/off switch** `[implemented 2026-10-13]`, every provider card on Model Providers has an on/off switch beside its delete button. On, the card behaves as before: it tests the connection, lists models, probes LM Studio's `lms` CLI and polls a local server's status. Off, the card collapses to its name, address and a "disabled" chip, and Nekko Agent never contacts the provider: no connection test, no model list, no `lms` probe, no runtime status or fit polling (both the TS host and the `nekkod` daemon answer those calls with an empty result for a disabled provider), so opening the page cannot wake a stopped LM Studio. A disabled provider is also left out of every model picker and, as before, cannot run a chat. Only an explicit off counts: older entries saved without the flag stay on. Stored as the existing `ProviderConfig.enabled`; no new settings. `[Implemented; host unit test, nekkod integration test, renderer build. Desktop visual check pending.]`
- **Subscription sign-in** `[planned]`, one-click OAuth sign-in that lets a **Claude Pro/Max** or **ChatGPT Plus/Pro/Business** subscription drive the app instead of a metered API key: browser authorize + loopback callback on desktop, a paste-the-code fallback for headless/remote, and one-click **import from the Claude Code / Codex CLI** for anyone already signed in there. Tokens refresh silently, are stored mode-0600 in the data dir, never enter settings export, and a lapsed session asks for re-auth rather than failing mid-chat. Subscription is the encouraged path for anyone holding one (no per-token fees); API keys stay fully supported as the quiet alternative, and provider cards badge which kind of auth they use. **Caveat the UI states plainly**: these flows use the vendor's own first-party CLI OAuth client, which vendor terms scope to their own apps, so availability is at the vendor's discretion and can change. OpenRouter joins the sign-in surface with the same one-click shape but a different thing underneath: its OAuth is PKCE without a client id, scopes, or echoed state, and the exchange returns a permanent API key (revoked, never refreshed), so it bills per token like any API key rather than reporting subscription windows. `[updated 2026-10-02]` A sign-in provider's model list is **live, not compiled in**: the ChatGPT provider reads the same picker catalog the Codex CLI reads (`/codex/models`, filtered by the account's plan and current rollouts), so a model released after this build appears on its own and a model the account cannot run never appears. The built-in list survives only as an offline fallback, refreshed to the current generation rather than retired ids that the backend now rejects.
- **Subscription limits visibility** `[shipped 2026-09-08]`, sign-in providers show the real plan state, not a guess: a chat-header **limits chip** reads the binding window's utilization and a hover/click **popover** lists every window (the rolling 5-hour session, the 7-day weekly, and any model-scoped windows like a weekly Sonnet/Opus cap) with its used-percent and reset time, plus plan type and credits where the provider reports them. Claude limits come from the `anthropic-ratelimit-unified-*` response headers every subscription-mode reply already carries (with an on-demand `/api/oauth/usage` read for first paint and refresh); ChatGPT limits come from a throttled `GET /wham/usage` poll. The provider card in Models shows the same breakdown. These are live provider-side reads, plainly labelled as such, never extrapolated.
- **Per-model pricing + session cost** `[shipped 2026-09-08]`, wherever a model is chosen it shows a **list-price estimate** (`$input/$output per 1M tokens`) so the cost of a pick is visible before it runs: subscription models read **"Included in plan"** with the equivalent list price muted beside it, metered models show the dollar estimate, local models $0. The chat header carries a **running session total** built from the same usage log the Cost panel reads, so metered spend accumulates live while subscription usage reports as included rather than billed. Prices are published list-price estimates, not a bill.
- **Models page** `[shipped]`, server cards (add/connect ollama/lmstudio/vllm/cloud), model list, model favorites, and per-provider server management. Each local server card shows which models are **loaded** in memory (Ollama via `/api/ps`, LM Studio via its native `/api/v0/models` state, vLLM = always resident), auto-refreshing while connected. Per-model **load/unload** is available for Ollama (native HTTP) and for LM Studio (via its `lms` CLI, for a local instance with the CLI installed). A **Stop server** action terminates the server process (killing whatever listens on its port); it stays the unload path for vLLM and for a remote/CLI-less LM Studio, which have no per-model unload. It appears **only on a local provider that is actually connected**, since an offline server has no process to stop and a greyed-out button just invites a click that can't work. A model's **load** chip is visibly interactive (accent tint and ring on hover), and a loaded one reports "loaded" at rest and offers "unload" under the cursor, so one chip carries both the state and the action. `[updated 2026-10-01]` A cloud card whose connection test fails on a missing or dead credential no longer dead-ends on the red message: kinds that have a sign-in flow (Claude, ChatGPT, OpenRouter) offer **Reconnect** beside it, which runs that sign-in in place. The fresh credential replaces the stale one on the same card (an api-key Claude/ChatGPT card converts to subscription auth, OpenRouter mints a fresh metered key), and the card re-tests immediately so an "Offline" badge cannot linger over a connection that works again. Usage analytics (tokens over time, per model/provider) now live in the Command Center.
- **Runtime control plane** `[shipped 2026-09-09]`, local model servers are started, stopped, and driven from their card rather than merely observed. Ollama is supervised as a child process we own (so it stops with its own handle and never outlives the app); LM Studio uses its real `lms server start|stop` lifecycle; **vLLM is connect-existing only** and says so, because it serves one model per process configured entirely at launch and no machine here can verify a spawn path. What each runtime offers is declared as **capabilities** rather than inferred from its name, so the UI never presents a control a server cannot honour, and a future bundled engine slots in without UI changes. Stopping a process **Nekko Agent did not start** asks for confirmation first and names what it would kill, replacing the previous silent kill-whatever-holds-the-port behaviour; a process we started stops without ceremony. A failed start surfaces the server's own captured output ("address already in use" and "command not found" are different problems), and a runtime that is not installed says so instead of offering a Start button that cannot work.
- **VRAM fit planner** `[shipped 2026-09-09]`, the answer to "will this model fit, where will it run, and why", *before* the load. Weights plus KV cache (`2 x layers x kvHeads x headDim x context x bytesPerElement x parallelSlots`) plus runtime overhead, measured against one memory pool, producing a verdict (fits / tight / spills / will-not-load / **unknown**), the estimated layers that land on the CPU, structured reasons carrying the numbers that drove them, and re-planned one-click fixes. Three honesty rules are load-bearing. **`unknown` is a first-class verdict**: capacity is never inferred from a model's name, so a missing layer count says so rather than producing a confident wrong number. **Unified memory is never double-counted**: on Apple Silicon the budget is system memory, not a card's VRAM plus RAM. And **multiple discrete GPUs are not pooled**, since without tensor parallelism a model must fit on one of them. Where a server reports less than the planner needs (LM Studio publishes no layer geometry), the projection is **partial rather than absent**: the real weights figure is still shown, a will-not-load verdict is still certain when the weights alone overflow the machine, and the missing terms are named. After a load the measured residency is reconciled against the projection and the residual feeds a per-runtime calibration record, so the overhead constant improves with use instead of staying a guess nobody can check.
- **The Nekko engine** `[shipped 2026-09-16]`, the model server Nekko Agent runs itself, so a fresh install can run a model with nothing else present. llama.cpp is acquired from its own releases on explicit consent, with the build chosen from what the GPU probe actually found rather than from the platform (CUDA only where there is an NVIDIA reading, Vulkan for an unidentified GPU, CPU otherwise), and a `llama-server` the user already has is used instead when they point at one. Models come from a short curated list or a Hugging Face search, with each build's real download size, and transfers that **resume**, cancel for real, and land only once the file parses as a GGUF. Serving is **one OpenAI-compatible address** fronting a `llama-server` per loaded model, so several models are resident at once, a request for one that is not loaded loads it, an idle one is evicted, and the address survives every load and unload; port, network binding, API key, CORS, resident limit, idle timeout and models folder are all configurable, with the safe defaults left in place and the two settings that can expose the machine saying so. The controls come in **two surfaces over one state**: one slider asking how much of the machine the model may use, solved through the fit planner into real settings and explained in memory rather than in invented tokens per second, and the full llama.cpp flag set behind it, each moving the other. Reading each file's own GGUF header makes the projection **exact** for a model the engine holds, where another server's model leaves terms unknown. Verified end to end on an RTX 5090: engine installed, Qwen3 4B downloaded, loaded at 32k context taking a measured 7.94 GB against a 7.75 GB projection, and answering through the router at 162-292 tokens per second.
  - **The NVIDIA engine actually uses the GPU on Windows** `[fixed 2026-10-12; unit-tested, repair not yet run on a live machine]`. The CUDA build needs a separate runtime-library archive, and every llama.cpp release carries a Linux one beside the Windows one with the same toolkit and arch. The installer matched on toolkit and arch alone and took the Linux archive, so Windows got `.so` files it cannot load: `llama-server` started, reported healthy, found no CUDA device and ran every model on the CPU, so chats sat on "working" for minutes per reply with no error anywhere. The runtime archive is now matched on platform, toolkit and arch together, and a missing match fails the install with a message rather than taking another platform's. An install already in that state is recognised from its files (a managed Windows CUDA build with no `cublas64_*.dll` beside it) and Model server shows, in words without CUDA in them, that models are running on the processor, with a **Repair engine** button that reinstalls the same build. A reinstall unpacks into a clean folder, cleared only after the new archive has downloaded so a failed download leaves the old engine in place, and the server is stopped only at that moment. Not covered yet: a CUDA build that has its libraries but still finds no GPU (driver too old for the toolkit); that would need the engine's own device list, which is a process launch and too slow for the status poll.
- **Decision models** `[runtime, UI and agent tool shipped 2026-10-01; no hosted export yet]`, a decision model answers named, typed questions about a text or JSON state (`choice`, `score`, `noul`) with calibrated probabilities in one forward pass and never generates text. The engine daemon runs **Laya** (open weights, Apache 2.0) natively on ONNX Runtime, on the GPU where it can (DirectML on Windows, CoreML on macOS, CUDA on Linux builds that include it) and on the CPU otherwise, reporting which it used. Requests and answers follow TypeSafe Jev's shape, on `POST /v1/decisions` and `/v1/systemone` at the engine's address, so a Jev client can point at the local engine. Its answers match the reference `laya` package's on a 60-request golden set (identical model input, same answer on every question in fp16 and fp32). Only the English checkpoint runs today, and only from an export made with the documented recipe, since the one published export is wrong; the base model is near chance on some benchmarks zero-shot and ships over-confident, which the answers do not hide. See [docs/decision-models.md](docs/decision-models.md). In the app, Nekko Server's **Decisions** tab lists Laya (its download stays off until a correct export is hosted, and says why), takes an export folder the user adds (read in place; removing it never deletes files), loads it at a chosen precision and shows the accelerator in use, keeps a TypeSafe key for the hosted **Jev**, and has a Try-it panel with probability bars for either. While either one is available, chat agents get a `decide` tool that sends typed questions to it, so routing, triage and yes/no calls come back as probabilities rather than the chat model's guess.
- **Model Server, split from Model Providers** `[shipped 2026-09-17]`, the two questions that had been sharing one page are now two tabs. **Model Server** is this machine: the engine, its model library, the folders those models come from, the address it serves on, and the local API/CLI/MCP surface that lets other software drive Nekko Agent. **Model Providers** is where models come from elsewhere: cloud keys, subscriptions, and servers running on this or another machine. The engine's library is no longer hidden behind its install gate either, so a machine with twenty models already on it says so before anyone downloads a runtime.
- **Models from other apps' folders** `[shipped 2026-09-17]`, a GGUF is a GGUF whoever downloaded it, so the library reads the folders the rest of the machine already keeps models in. On first run the usual locations for Ollama, LM Studio, Jan, GPT4All, llama.cpp, KoboldCpp, LocalAI, Bionic, text-generation-webui and the Hugging Face cache (which is what vLLM serves from) are probed, and each one holding models becomes a row. **Ollama needs its own reader**: it files models as content-addressed blobs with no extension, so its manifests are parsed and models appear under the name you would type at `ollama run`. The **Folders** tab is the whole list and every part of it is editable — path, on/off, add, remove — with what each contributes shown beside it. Borrowed folders are **read, never written**: their models load like ours, wear the name of the app they came from, and offer no delete, because the file belongs to that app. Ids stay unique across folders and our own models keep their bare ids, so nothing already saved in a preset or typed into another tool stops resolving.
- **One models surface** `[shipped 2026-09-30]`, the library and the catalog stopped being two tabs and became one search. The same box filters what is on this machine and queries Hugging Face, with the installed models above and the catalog below, so the answer to "do I have qwen" is never on the other tab. The catalog explains itself where the confusion was: parameter counts read as billions of parameters with the tradeoff stated, every model's smallest build is checked against this machine's memory pool and chips **fits here** / **tight fit** / **needs more memory** (a deliberate rough check, not a promise, since the exact working set is only known once the file is on disk), the curated starter list carries a **recommended** pick, and the model page marks the build most people want (Q4_K_M, or the lightest that fits) with one-line notes on what each quantization trades.
- **A library that knows what each file is** `[shipped 2026-09-30]`, the model list stops pretending every GGUF is a chat model. Each file's declared architecture classifies it (**chat**, **vision**, **embedding**, **image gen**, **audio**, **draft head**), the badge is on the row with a filter for each kind plus **Can't run here**, and a model llama.cpp cannot serve gets an explanation instead of a Load button: image generation needs a diffusion engine, speech needs a speech engine, a draft head only runs attached to its full model, and an unreadable file needs re-downloading. The list gains an **In memory** section naming what is resident and when it will evict, an **on start** toggle per model that autoloads it with its saved preset when the engine starts (failures logged, never blocking the rest), a **keep it loaded** switch and per-model idle timeout that can change without a reload, and a last-load-error line translated from llama.cpp's log into a fixable cause. Vision rows report whether their `mmproj` projector is present and fetch it, the tokenizer config and any `.jinja` or JSON-embedded chat template are picked up beside the model or from a Nekko-owned companions folder so borrowed folders stay read-only, and a missing split shard or companion fails before `llama-server` ever spawns with a message naming what is absent. `[updated 2026-10-01]` Rows lead with the model's **name** with its file path beneath as secondary text, and the list **groups by the folder each model came from**, so a shelf of twenty models reads as libraries, not paths. A model whose saved settings would not fit entirely on the GPU is marked in place, and a resident model says where it actually runs (**on GPU · 61/61 layers**, **GPU + CPU**, **on CPU**), so the reason a reply is slow is printed on the row instead of discovered by benchmark.
- **Models that start on the first try** `[shipped 2026-09-30]`, a downloaded GGUF no longer fails at load for want of the small files beside it. Catalog downloads pull the companion files a build actually needs (the mmproj projector for vision models, split shards, tokenizer config, external chat templates) and the Downloads tab shows them as **one download per model** (combined progress, rate and time left, one cancel for every file) with a row per file naming what it is and why it came along: model weights (part N of M for split builds), vision projector, tokenizer, chat template, model config `[updated 2026-10-09; verified with unit tests and an isolated fixture render, not yet in a live download]`, the server resolves them from the model's own folder at load time and passes `--mmproj` and `--chat-template-file` itself, and a missing shard or companion surfaces as an actionable error naming what is absent rather than llama.cpp's raw "may need a JSON file" failure.
- **A model's own page** `[shipped 2026-09-17]`, opening a model from the catalog takes the whole view instead of unfolding a drawer inside a list. It carries the publisher's model card, download and like counts, when the repo was first published and last touched, its declared task, license and base model, every tag it carries, and the full ladder of builds with each one's real transfer size and whether it is already in the library. The card is parsed into elements we construct rather than injected as markup, images are dropped rather than fetched from third-party hosts, and HTML the publisher wrote is reduced to its text: it is prose from a stranger's repository and is treated as such.
- **Real context windows** `[shipped 2026-09-28]`, every current Claude model holds **1M tokens** (Haiku 4.5 holds 200k; `[updated 2026-10-10]` Haiku 5 onwards holds 1M, #413), and the gauge, the Context Inspector and the workspace card all say so; the catalog had claimed 200k for all of them. The count estimates what is actually replayed (text, tool arguments and tool output, but not stored reasoning that providers omit), so a long agentic run reads as its real size instead of a few thousand tokens; it remains an estimate, not the provider's exact accounting. Attached files and guidelines reach the model in full rather than as their 160-character previews. When an estimate exceeds the listed window but the provider continues responding, the warning does not claim a hard failure.
- **Effort that follows the model** `[shipped 2026-09-28]`, the effort menu offers the levels the chat's model actually has: Anthropic's five (**Low, Medium, High, Extra high, Max**) on Claude models that take an effort level, and the three temperature steps everywhere else. **Default** follows the model and names the level it resolves to, because that is not the same everywhere (Opus 5.5 defaults to Medium, Opus 5 to High). A saved level a model lacks runs as the nearest one it has, and the menu shows what will be sent rather than what was once picked. Claude Opus 5.5 is in the catalog. `[updated 2026-10-02]` On the ChatGPT subscription provider the level goes out as the Codex backend's `reasoning.effort` rung: it rejects `temperature` and `max_output_tokens` outright (a 400 that used to fail every ChatGPT turn), so those are never sent and there is no output cap on that provider. `[updated 2026-10-02]` The same care on the OpenAI-compatible providers (OpenAI API key, OpenRouter, LM Studio, vLLM, llama.cpp, generic servers): an OpenAI reasoning id (o-series, gpt-5 and newer, codex, gpt-oss) sends `reasoning_effort` plus `max_completion_tokens` on the OpenAI API and `reasoning.effort` through OpenRouter, never `temperature`. Local servers keep the sampling fields whatever the id says, and a stricter server that still refuses a field gets its way once per reply: the blamed field is dropped, or renamed to the one the error suggests, the request is retried, and the adjustment is remembered so every later chat with that model sends the right shape the first time. `[updated 2026-10-05]` The slider's look mirrors the level: a soft solid glow hugs only the filled run left of the thumb and grows as the rung climbs, and the top rung draws a small set of static speed lines trailing off the thumb. `[updated 2026-10-10]` Haiku 5 onwards gets Anthropic's five levels; it had shown the three temperature steps while its requests already carried an effort level (#413).
- **Capacity across every provider** `[shipped 2026-09-28, expanded 2026-09-30]`, a collapsible **Capacity** panel above the resource meters lists every cloud provider that can be measured, with each usage window it reports (bar, percent, time to reset), its credit balance where it has one, and a plain status where it reports nothing. Reads come from documented, authorized endpoints for the credential the provider actually holds: OAuth token reads for signed-in providers (Claude, ChatGPT, OpenRouter sign-in), and for an OpenRouter **API key** the same `GET /api/v1/key` read the key itself is authorized for (`[updated]`). Collapsed, it still shows the single most-spent window anywhere. A provider whose credential has no authorized usage read (a plain OpenAI key: the usage API requires a separate org admin key) is labelled **no usage API**, distinguished from zero use and from a provider still being read, never an invented percentage. The panel stays read-only; acting on the data is the opt-in **Follow capacity & cost** switch in Auto mode (see *Model auto-mode, with profiles*).
- **Resource monitors** `[shipped]`, four live monitors, **CPU** load, **memory** use, **GPU** utilization, and **VRAM**, surfaced in two places that never fight for the same corner. A quiet always-on **monitor chip** sits bottom-right on **tabs other than Command Center** (one segment per enabled monitor, plus a VRAM micro-bar); hovering it opens the panel where each monitor is switched on or off and a per-GPU breakdown is shown. The chat's context panel carries the **full monitoring section** at its foot (CPU with core count and processor name, memory used/total with %, GPU utilization, VRAM used/total with % and per-device used/free). While that section is on screen the chip **warps into it**, shrinking, blurring and fading along a path that ends on the section (which answers with a one-shot glow), and flies back out when the panel closes, so the same numbers are never stacked on top of each other. **Switching a monitor off stops the work behind it**: the renderer polls only the probes the enabled monitors need, so turning GPU/VRAM off spawns no GPU probe and turning CPU/memory off samples no CPU times; with all four off the chip reads "Monitors off" and nothing is polled at all. CPU/memory come from `os` (portable, no native module, load measured as a delta between samples like a task manager). The GPU probe is **per platform**: `nvidia-smi` on Windows and Linux, and on **macOS** the accelerator driver's own counters read out of the IO registry (`ioreg -c IOAccelerator`), which needs no admin rights, unlike `powermetrics`. A Mac therefore reports its real GPU ("Apple M1 Max (32-core GPU)", live utilization) instead of the old "No NVIDIA GPU detected". Because Apple Silicon has **no dedicated VRAM**, the memory meter there renames itself **GPU memory**, measures the driver's current allocation against the unified pool, and says "unified with system RAM" so nobody reads it as a discrete card's VRAM; the dock names the probes it used (`os + ioreg`, `os + nvidia-smi`). A machine whose GPU can't be read says so plainly rather than naming a vendor it never checked for. Preferences persist in settings (`AppSettings.monitors`); one shared sampler feeds both surfaces so nothing is probed twice per tick.
- **Reasoning-model streaming** `[shipped]`, `reasoning_content` deltas (OpenAI-compatible) and `message.thinking` (Ollama) render into a collapsed, persisted "Thought for Ns" summary above each assistant answer; the full reasoning remains expandable.
- **Thinking toggle** `[shipped]`, for reasoning-capable models the metrics bar's "thinking" indicator becomes a per-chat **on/off toggle** (`session.thinking`) that turns reasoning on or off for that chat. It maps to each server's native knob, Ollama `think`, OpenAI-compatible `chat_template_kwargs.enable_thinking` (Qwen3 and friends), and is only sent to local servers that understand it. Non-reasoning models keep the passive "streamed reasoning this turn" indicator.

### Chat & cowork (unified surface)

- **Agents (split windows, no tabs)** `[shipped 2026-09-16]` `[renamed 2026-10-03]`, the Chat surface (reached from the **Agent** tab, which wears the Aphelion cat avatar in the rail and sits first in it, above Command Center; the rail is headed by the **cat mark** (the brand icon, see *App icon*), with the "Nekko Agent" wordmark beside it only where no title bar carries it) is a list of **agents**, each one a whole arrangement of windows you switch to as a unit. (They were called workspaces until 2026-10-03; the UI now says agents everywhere, "New agent", "No agents yet", "Close this agent", while the code keeps the workspace name.) An agent opens around a chat and grows whatever that work needs, a file, a terminal, a browser, a file explorer, a second agent, and every window in it is on screen at once: there is **no tab strip anywhere**, so opening a file never costs you sight of the agent that opened it. Windows are arranged as a split tree, up to **8 across and 8 down**, resized by dragging the divider between any two (pointer **capture**, so the drag keeps tracking over a window's own iframe or terminal; a focused divider also moves with the arrow keys) and rearranged by dragging a window's title strip onto any side of another (the target lights the band it would fill; the nearest edge wins, so each corner belongs to its shorter side). A new window is added through the **split compass**: hovering a window's **+** draws that window as a glyph with a target on each of its four sides and a line running out to each, the line under the direction you point at running faster and brighter; pick a side, then pick what goes there (chat, terminal, files, browser). Directions the 8×8 ceiling has used up are visibly spent rather than silently dead.
  - The left sidebar lists workspaces, grouped by project in collapsible folder sections (animated chevron + item-count badge when collapsed, accent-highlighted active workspace, spawned sub-agents nested under their parent). Project-less work sits in a **General** group kept at the **top** of the sidebar (hidden when empty), and a **new chat auto-files under the project it's about** the first time it runs, inferred from its attached folders and first prompt (a confident, non-ambiguous match only; a general chat stays under General). `[updated 2026-10-01]` The sidebar itself resizes horizontally by dragging its divider, bounded between 200 and 480px so it can be a strip of icons or half the window but never either extreme; arrow keys step it on the focused handle, double-click resets it, and the width is remembered between sessions.
  - **Each card says what its agent is running**, in dense lines and without being opened: title with live state (working / waiting on you / stopped) and time since the last reply; **source / model** ("Claude / Opus 5.5") and how full that chat's **context window** is (`155k/1M`, coloured by fill); the **branch** (with the worktree glyph when the chat works in a linked git worktree) and upstream drift, then a small separator and **each PR the chat made as its own link** (`#264`, coloured by state: open, draft, merged, closed), including the PR the checked-out branch is going into. Past three PRs they fold into a pull-request glyph and the count; hovering it (or clicking, for keyboard and touch) lists every PR with its title and state, each separately clickable. Following a PR link never opens the chat behind it. The project folder is not repeated, since the card already sits under its project's heading, and the uncommitted-file count is gone from the card; sub-agents and window count follow when there are any. The completion check that appears on hover is a size up so it is easy to hit. `[updated 2026-10-03]` The chat window's own header carries the same worktree, branch and PR, from the same cached git read, so the card and the window never disagree. The card uses one quiet surface rather than a different tinted background for each fact. This replaced a single run-info panel at the foot of the sidebar that could only ever describe whichever chat was open. `[updated 2026-09-29]` `[updated 2026-10-02]` Ages read in words that cannot be misread, "5 mins ago" and "3 months ago" rather than "5m" and "3mo", on the card, on the workspace rows and anywhere else a relative time is printed, because a bare m meant minutes in one place and months in the next.
  - Each chat window is independent, with its own provider/model picker, streaming, and Context Inspector, so agents run in parallel. The chat itself opens on a friendly empty state and a **unified composer card**: textarea, attach menu, Queue, and the **send control is Aphelion herself** (the cat avatar, no button fill, lifting on hover) in one rounded surface with a whole-card focus ring. `[updated 2026-10-03]` In the composer, **Tab on a list line indents it** two spaces and **Shift+Tab** takes them back (bullets, numbered items and task lines alike, every selected line at once), which is how a bullet becomes a sub-bullet; on any other line Tab still moves focus as a form expects. A chat that references a project shows a project chip on its window's title strip, so windows from different projects are tellable apart. `[updated 2026-10-02]` The strip reads name, then a `/`, then where it is working (the project chip, the git worktree and branch): the slash leads the location rather than trailing the title, and it stays hidden on a window with no location to name.
- **A resizable composer** `[shipped 2026-09-28]`, the line between the conversation and the composer drags: up gives the message box room for a long prompt, down gives it back to the transcript, which never shrinks below a readable minimum. The size is remembered; double-click the line to go back to growing with what you type.
- **The composer speaks Markdown back** `[shipped 2026-10-01]`, the message bubbles had always rendered Markdown, but the box you type it into did not, so the same syntax looked different on either side of send. Now a mirrored overlay behind the transparent-text textarea styles it while you write: `# ` heading lines, `- ` and `1.` list markers, `**bold**`, `*italic*`, `~~strike~~`, inline `code`, and `#tags`, with the caret still visible and the overlay's scrolling locked to the textarea's, so what you write previews exactly what the bubble will render.
- **Narration stays in the conversation** `[shipped 2026-09-28]`, what the model says between tool calls is written out as part of the conversation instead of folding into the collapsed steps as a "Said" row, and each stretch of steps between two such messages is its own group, so a long run reads as what it said, what it did, what it said next.
- **Questions one at a time** `[shipped 2026-09-28]`, when the agent stops to ask, each question is its own step with Next and Back, in the shape its answer takes (single choice, multi-select, free text), with options as a vertical list rather than wrapping chips.
- **Context capacity and continuation** `[shipped 2026-10-03]`, workspace cards show estimated transcript usage and update every four seconds while a chat is running. The in-chat capacity warning begins at 95%. At that threshold, automatic compaction starts after the active reply completes, summarizes older conversation, and continues in the same session while retaining recent turns where possible. While it runs, the banner shows a progress bar ("Summarizing part 2 of 4") and two buttons: **Send summary to new chat** (the summary, plus the recent turns, starts a new chat with the same project, folders and model, which opens when ready; this chat is left untouched) and **Stop compacting** (nothing changes). Outside a run the banner offers Compact now, Send summary to new chat, and a blank New chat. Compacting never deletes anything: the earlier turns stay readable above a divider ("76 earlier messages compacted"), the latest summary sits open below it, and from then on the model is sent only that summary and what follows it, never the older turns (both the in-process loop and the engine daemon's loop). The context gauge and workspace card count only what is still sent. A long chat that is mostly tool output compacts in minutes rather than never: tool output is clipped to its head and tail before summarizing, chunks are summarized a few at a time at low effort, and each summary call has a four-minute limit that fails with a clear message instead of hanging.
  - **Running out of context does not stop the work** `[implemented 2026-10-15; unit, golden and parity tests pass; not yet exercised against a live small-window model]`. Three layers, cheapest first, the same in the in-process loop and the engine daemon's loop:
    1. *Stay inside the window during a reply.* Each turn learns the window the model was actually **loaded** with, not the id's nominal size: LM Studio's loaded context, Ollama's `/api/ps`, the engine's and OpenRouter's advertised context, else the id-based guess. Once a request would pass 80% of that window, the oldest bulky tool outputs (over 600 characters, never the newest four messages) are replaced in what is *sent* with a one-line note saying how long they were and that the tool can be run again, until the prompt is back under 60%. The saved transcript keeps everything. The cut point only moves forward, so consecutive requests share a prefix and prompt caches keep working.
    2. *Retry an overflow once, slimmer.* If the provider still rejects the request as too long for its context (OpenAI's "maximum context length", Anthropic's "prompt is too long", llama.cpp's "exceeds the available context size", and similar), the same request is sent once more with every eligible older tool output left out, before the reply is given up.
    3. *Compact and carry on.* If the reply still ends on an overflow, the host compacts the chat and sends it on by itself with a visible note asking the model to continue from the summary. This is bounded to two automatic continuations in a row (reset by any reply that finishes); past that, or if compaction fails, the chat says why and waits. A reply that finishes with the transcript at 85% of the window is compacted before the next one, even when no chat pane is open (agent watches, queues, the phone). Incognito chats, sub-agents, goal runs, archived chats and chats with queued prompts are never compacted or continued automatically.
    The banner for a reply that outgrew its window says "Out of context" in the paused (not failure) colour and explains it carries on by itself; it clears as soon as the continuation starts. Compaction sizes its summary chunks from the loaded window too, so compacting an 8k local model no longer sends it 128k-sized chunks.
- **Runs that keep going when you look away** `[shipped 2026-09-28]`, every chat's live turn is followed app-wide rather than by the window showing it, so switching workspaces no longer drops output, and coming back shows the run exactly where it is. The status under a streaming reply says what it is doing in a few words ("Reading ChatPane.tsx", "Running npm") instead of "Streaming", and sub-agents appear in the plan rail while they work.
- **An editable plan rail** `[shipped 2026-09-28]`, each step of the decoded plan can be typed into and edited in place, dragged to reorder, and assigned to a sub-agent, and an assigned step reaches the agent as an instruction to delegate it. `[updated 2026-09-30]` The rail now also carries the **agent's own plan**: after reading the request the model reports the plan it is actually working through (a live checklist, distinct from the user's editable notes), instead of the rail restating the prompt as bullets.
- **Workspace titles that name the work** `[shipped 2026-09-30]`, a new chat's first reply quietly asks the model for a short task-shaped title ("Fix the CSV import") and applies it to the workspace card and sidebar row, so a list of chats reads as what each one is doing rather than the prompt's first forty characters. It runs after the first turn, never blocks or fails the reply it rode in on, and a title the user typed is never overwritten.
- **In-chat pull requests** `[shipped]`, a chat "has" a PR when a GitHub PR URL shows up in its transcript (the agent runs `gh pr create`, the URL lands in tool output, or the user pastes one). Those PRs surface as **cards inline in the chat**, as **links** on the chat's sidebar card (see the workspace card above), and as **badges** on the chat header (open count, "PR ready" when approved + green, merged count). An open PR's card keeps its info (repo/number, state, diff counts, checks) on the left and parks a **modern, right-aligned action cluster**: **Approve** (a solid green gradient primary), **Decline** (a quiet red ghost), **Review** (a bordered secondary that opens the diff), a subtle violet **Merge**, and a compact "open on GitHub" arrow, each behind a one-click confirm; the info-left / actions-right split keeps the card slim. A merged PR's card becomes a purple, star-dusted "PR merged" banner. `[updated 2026-10-05]` The banner is readable in both themes: every piece of text on it (repository and number, title, the merged label) keeps at least 4.5:1 contrast against the part of the gradient behind it, in light and dark and in each theme preset. The purple wash and star dust stay, but the text colours follow the theme instead of being fixed to light violet, which made the left side of the banner unreadable on a light background. The merged PR badges and links elsewhere in the app meet the same bar. **Review** opens the PR's diff as a window beside the chat (Devin-style, per-file, syntax-tinted). Live state, the diff, and the actions run through the **`gh` CLI**, falling back to the **GitHub REST API** with the GitHub connector's token when `gh` isn't installed; nothing about PRs is tracked or stored by Nekko Agent beyond what the transcript already contains.
- **Hover-expanding nav rail** `[shipped]`, the left icon rail expands over the content on hover (52 → 188px since 2026-10-14; was 64 → 200px, springy width + label fade/slide animation) to reveal each destination's name, and collapses back when the pointer leaves; keyboard focus (Tab) expands it too. Its right divider is absent while collapsed for a quieter edge and appears only with the expanded rail, so it never crosses the mascot at rest.
- **Experimental surfaces, opt-in** `[shipped 2026-09-08]`, a new **Settings → Experimental** card holds a toggle per in-progress surface, keeping the sidebar to the stable set by default. **Model training**, **Design board**, and **Memory** each hide their nav destination (rail, phone bottom bar, and the command palette's navigation entries) until switched on; turning one off hides the tab again, and a surface you're standing on lands you on the Command Center instead of a dead end. The flags persist in settings (`AppSettings.experimental`) and apply across every edition, desktop, web, and Docker share the gate.
- **Terminals** `[shipped]`, persistent shell sessions rendered as Warp-style command **blocks** (command + streamed output + exit code + duration). Backed by one long-lived host shell per terminal (PowerShell on Windows, `$SHELL`/bash elsewhere) so cwd/env persist across commands; marker-delimited blocks capture status with no native PTY (honors the no-native-modules rule). Command history (↑/↑), best-effort interrupt, scrollback restore on tab reattach. New-terminal shortcut (Ctrl/⌘+`, the terminal key from VS Code, Cursor, and Zed; Ctrl/⌘+J still works).
- **Workspace prompt surface** `[shipped 2026-10-02]`, an empty chat shows a scrollable model catalog directly in the conversation, with recent models, starred models and the remaining provider lists, plus the multimodal/image choice. The prompt rail shows only the agent's published plan, not a second decomposition of the user's request; changes to the agent's plan are requested by follow-up. Finished sub-agents leave the live workspace list while their chat history remains on disk. Agent command logs are marked finished when their turn ends; the TypeScript fallback shell kills its process tree on Stop or timeout, matching daemon-owned bash cancellation.
- **Sub-agents & orchestration** `[shipped]`, a `spawn_agent` tool lets an agent delegate a scoped sub-task to a fresh child session (own context, same project, inherits the parent's tool-execution mode). An **orchestration strategy** (Settings) governs delegation: `solo` (no sub-agents, `spawn_agent` is withheld), `balanced` (delegate only clearly separable/heavy work, default), or `swarm` (act as orchestrator, proactively decompose into parallel sub-agents). The strategy shapes the system prompt and tool availability; **max nesting depth** and an advisory **parallel** count are configurable. Children stream their own events, and the Command Center renders the full **swarm as a recursive tree** under each agent (live status, descendant counts, click-to-open) and reports the final answer back as the tool result.
- **Streaming chat** `[shipped]`, markdown + code rendering, persisted collapsed reasoning summaries, expandable "Used `<tool>` tool" cards, approval prompts (the full command can be read by hovering it or with a Show full command toggle; implemented, visually unverified), and speech-bubble messages. A turn's **concluding text always surfaces as the answer bubble**, even when the run ends on a tool-calling step (cut short by the loop detector, an abort, or a model that concludes in the same message as its final tool call), so the wrap-up no longer folds into the collapsed activity group and vanishes. **Markdown covers both sides of the conversation**, the user's own messages render it too, since people type dashed lists, `code`, and links into the composer and expect them formatted: bullet and numbered lists (nested by indentation, and recognised even when the run is glued to the sentence above it, no blank line needed), headings, block quotes, rules, pipe tables, fenced code, and inline bold / italic / strikethrough / code / links plus bare-URL autolinking. Single newlines stay line breaks, and snake_case identifiers or `2 * 3 * 4` arithmetic are never mistaken for emphasis.
- **Composer attachments & the + menu** `[shipped]`, paste images directly into the composer or use the **+** menu to add photos, session files, or project folders. The + menu's **Skill** row **expands its skills as a side flyout on hover** (no click needed): every skill this chat can run (built-ins plus installed marketplace skills, starred ones first, each with its description) plus an **Add skill** row that opens the Skills tab. Picking one arms it in the composer exactly as `/name` does, so skills are discoverable by hovering rather than only by knowing to type a slash; a short close-delay lets the pointer cross to the flyout, and Escape closes the flyout before it closes the menu. Pasted and selected images travel with the user message as data URLs and are encoded for multimodal requests by OpenAI-compatible, Anthropic, and Ollama providers. Attachments **live inside the composer card**, a thumbnail strip across its top separated from the textarea by a hairline, so they can never cover the controls above it. Any chat image, thumbnail or full-size preview, has a **right-click menu**: *Copy image* (put on the clipboard as PNG) and *Save image…* (download it). Escape dismisses that menu without closing the preview behind it.
- **Saved drafts & ready-to-type composer** `[shipped]`, an unsent message is never lost. A window that is closed is unmounted, so the composer's text **and its pending image attachments** are parked per chat and restored when you come back, whether you switched to another workspace, left the Chat view, or quit and reopened the app. Sending or queueing clears the parked draft. Opening a chat also puts the cursor straight in the composer (after any restored text), so a new agent is ready to type into the moment it appears.
- **Metrics bar** `[shipped]`, context used/total with an **expanded by-source token tooltip** (a segmented usage bar plus each source's token count and share of the window, a **Free space** row, and the total, Claude-Code style), tokens/sec, a thinking on/off toggle (reasoning models) or indicator, a live **session cost** total plus a per-model **price estimate**, a **subscription-limits chip** for sign-in providers, compact provider/model switching, and an effort cycle (low/normal/high → sampling temperature). The model picker and **⚡ Automate** control live here rather than in the chat header, with the picker immediately to the left of effort.
- **Model auto-mode, with profiles** `[shipped, profiles 2026-07-28]`, a per-chat **✨ Auto (pick best)** option in the model picker, plus an **Auto profile** chip beside it that says how hard Auto leans on capability: **Cheap** (always the smallest capable model, whatever you ask), **Normal** (reads the prompt: complex/coding work gets the strongest model the provider offers, a quick question gets a small fast one), **Quality** (always the strongest available). Favorited models break ties. Auto never picks a model that can't hold a conversation, speech recognition, embeddings, rerankers, OCR, and image models are filtered out, because a local server lists those from the same endpoint as its chat models. The strip shows a live **"→ &lt;model&gt;"** of what the next message will actually run on (with the prompt empty as well as typed) and explains the choice on hover. Persisted per chat (`session.autoModel`, `session.autoQuality`). The Auto profile menu also carries a per-chat **Follow capacity & cost** opt-in `[shipped 2026-09-30]`: when on, Auto may move a turn to an **equivalent or better** model on another provider, either because this provider is spent (a binding usage window rate-limited or past ~95%) or because a same-tier model elsewhere is **materially cheaper** (provider-published prices first, the static table second, local and plan-included providers counting as free, and an unpriced model can never be claimed cheap). The switched pick shows as **"→ &lt;model&gt; · &lt;provider&gt;"** and the turn announces why it moved ("the 5-hour limit is used up", "…does the same tier of work for free"); the send then runs through that provider so usage and cost stay attributable. A provider with no measured limits reads as full headroom but loses ties to a measured one, a candidate below the home pick's capability tier is never taken, and a step up says so. The switch happens before dispatch, never mid-stream, so the transcript and tool state are untouched; it is off by default and disabled in offline chats.
- **No silent model guess** `[shipped 2026-07-28]`, a chat without a model **asks** instead of inheriting one. The empty state reads "Pick a model to get started" with a button that opens the picker, a nudge above the composer says the chat needs a model, and the model chip itself reads "Choose a model" in accent. Nekko Agent no longer defaults to a provider's first model on connect, which used to silently select whatever a local server happened to list first (LM Studio's first entry is often `whisper-large-v3`, which can't answer a chat turn).
- **Flat grouped model picker** `[shipped]`, the composer's model menu lists **every provider's models in one flat list, grouped by provider** (no nested provider dropdown): a **★ Starred** section pinned on top, a star toggle on every row (persisted to the shared `favoriteModels` setting, same as the Models page), a filter box when the list is long, and implicit provider switching, picking a model from another provider switches the chat's provider in the same click. The pick is **remembered on the chat** (provider and model both), so it survives switching workspaces, closing the window, or restarting. The menu opens rightwards from the chip's own left edge, so it stays inside the pane instead of hanging over the sidebar. The strip above the composer **never wraps**: the model chip is the flexible member and truncates first, so a long local-model name can't push the controls onto a second line. If a chat has no usable model (its provider is offline or exposes none), sending **says so** rather than doing nothing, and keeps the draft. `[updated 2026-10-01]` Models served by the Nekko engine lead with their readable **name** rather than the file path that used to be their id, the path rides beneath as secondary text, and when they live in more than one folder the list groups them by where they came from, with a dot marking whichever is loaded.
- **Instrument strip, two rows and three control shapes** `[shipped]`, the settings above the composer are grouped by what they answer instead of reading as one long run of identical pills. **Row one is how this agent runs**: Mode, Tools, and **MCP** (the MCP-servers menu, see MCP client) on the left, and the two privacy switches (Offline, Incognito) parked on the right behind a hairline divider. **Row two is which brain it uses**: model, Thinking, Effort, with the Automate action on the right. Three deliberately different shapes carry the difference, **value pickers** are bordered fields with a muted label and a caret, **on/off switches** are quiet until hovered and show their state with color and a lit dot rather than a filled pill (Offline lights accent, Incognito plain ink, so only one accent state exists in the strip), and **actions** are plain icon buttons. Nothing wraps down to 640px wide.
- **Runaway-output backstops** `[shipped 2026-08-13]`, three things now bound a single response, because a local model that degenerates had nothing stopping it (one observed reply streamed 108,790 characters of reasoning, the same sentence 2,534 times, over nine minutes, and locked the window up). An **output cap** is sent to every **local** provider as its native limit (`max_tokens` / `num_predict`; default 8,192, 256–200,000 in Settings → Agent loop), where openai-compat and Ollama requests previously carried none at all and ran until they filled their own context window. `[updated 2026-10-03]` Cloud providers are **not capped**: the same 8k cap had been cutting long Claude and GPT replies off mid-sentence. OpenAI-compatible and Codex requests carry no cap; Anthropic's endpoint requires `max_tokens`, so it is sent the model's own ceiling (64k for Opus 4.5 onwards, Sonnet 3.7 onwards, Haiku 4.5, Fable and Mythos; `[updated 2026-10-10]` 128k for Haiku 5 onwards, #413; 32k for Opus 4 and 4.1 and for ids the app does not recognise), and a 400 that names a lower ceiling (`max_tokens: 64000 > 32000`) is learned for that model and the request goes again. A **repetition guard** watches each stream and cuts it off the moment the output collapses into a cycle, cancelling the request, keeping the useful head of the reply, and ending the turn instead of spending the rest of the step budget on the same loop. And streamed deltas are **batched into frames** rather than committing React state per token, with the live buffers capped, so a fast or runaway reply can't freeze the transcript.
- **Runs survive being cut off** `[shipped]`, a long agent run is checkpointed as it goes, so an interruption costs you the last step rather than all of them. Every completed step (the assistant message and each tool result) is **written to disk the moment it lands**, before the event even reaches the UI, so a run killed mid-flight by a timeout, a dropped connection, a crash, or a quit leaves its finished work on disk instead of only in memory. A reply whose stream breaks **keeps what the model had already written**, marked as cut off, rather than vanishing behind an error (a tool call it was still emitting is dropped: it never ran and its arguments may be truncated). Pressing **Stop** goes through the same path, so a stopped reply keeps its partial answer and reads as "Reply stopped", not as a failure. The failure notice then offers **Resume** as the primary action: the transcript is left exactly as it is, any tool call that never got to run is answered with a stand-in saying so (which is what makes an interrupted transcript valid to send again), and the model is told to continue from where it stopped and not repeat the work above. **Start over** remains beside it as the honest name for the destructive option, which re-answers the prompt from scratch and discards the run. (Before, a timeout on a long chat reported only "Reply failed", and the single **Retry** button truncated the chat back to the last user message, throwing away every step it had completed.)
- **Lifecycle hooks** `[shipped 2026-10-04]`, your own commands around the agent's actions (Settings → Hooks): **Before a tool** (`PreToolUse`: exit code 2, or `{"decision":"block","reason":"…"}` on stdout, blocks the call and the reason goes back to the model as the tool's error), **After a tool** (`PostToolUse`: whatever the command prints is appended to the tool result for the model, a linter's verdict, a reminder), and **When a reply ends** (`TurnEnd`: finished, failed or stopped, nothing waits on it). Each hook is a shell command that gets the event as JSON on stdin (event, chat id, tool name and input, the result for post-hooks, how the reply ended) and `NEKKO_HOOK_EVENT` in its environment, runs in the chat's workspace, has a tool-name matcher (a regular expression, empty for every tool) and a time limit (60 s by default); a hook that hangs or cannot start never blocks a tool. Since 2026-10-12 a turn the engine daemon drives runs its tool hooks in the daemon (`crates/nekko-tools` `hooks`, the same matching, payload and verdict rules), so tools keep running natively while hooks are on and a busy app no longer delays them; the rules are re-read for every call as before, and a hook that times out has its whole process tree stopped. `TurnEnd` hooks still run in the app (Claude Code's hooks, Codex's `notify`).
- **`fetch_url`** `[shipped 2026-10-04]`, the agent can read a public web page, a README, an API reference or a JSON endpoint without a browser window: one GET (30 s, 2 MB), HTML reduced to text with headings, lists, code and links kept (`label (href)`), JSON and plain text passed through, the first 20,000 characters by default (`max_chars` up to 100,000), binaries refused by name. No scripts, logins or searches; the browser tool remains for pages that need a real browser, and web search stays there until a search provider is chosen. Treated like reading a file: only Ask-everything mode asks first. Since 2026-10-12 a turn the engine daemon drives fetches in the daemon (`crates/nekko-web`), so a busy app no longer delays it; the text the model reads is held to the TS version by a golden set.
- **Desktop notifications** `[implemented; unit and fixture interaction tested, 2026-10-13; image inspection and native toast testing pending]`, enabled by default under **Settings > Notifications > Desktop notifications**. Electron main subscribes directly to the authenticated local engine event stream, independently of renderer visibility or suspension, and reconnects after endpoint changes. It reads current settings before posting a native notice for reply completion, non-Stop failure, a question or approval; relay-only echoes are excluded and notices deduplicate per chat/kind for five seconds. Clicking restores the UI and selects the session through `nekko-agent://session/open?id=...`. Quit disposes the listener and notices; Stop service stops the engine, not merely its window. Browser editions retain the renderer Notification API path and ask permission only when a notice is due. The original 2026-10-04 renderer-owned desktop delivery and Agent loop toggle placement are superseded. **Known review issue:** main currently treats every event as unattended, so focused visible chats can also notify; focused-chat suppression remains unfinished. Native OS delivery, click/focus behavior and platform permissions are unverified. Native notices contain chat titles and bounded question/error/approval text, so disabling this local setting is the opt-out; no new relay/cloud delivery is introduced.
- **Background processes** `[shipped 2026-10-04]`, the agent can start a dev server, a watcher or a long build and come back to it. `bash` is a round trip with a two-minute limit (and now says so), so agents used to hide long-running commands behind `start /b` or `&`, lose their output and leave them running; `start_process` runs the command detached from the turn under the same shell and guardrails as `bash`, returns an id at once, buffers its output (a megabyte, tail-kept) and mirrors it into the chat's agent terminal, `read_process` hands back what is new since the last read (optionally waiting up to two minutes for more, or listing the chat's processes), and `kill_process` stops one. Eight per chat; they stop with the chat's Stop and when the app quits (Claude Code's `run_in_background`, Codex's exec sessions). Since 2026-10-12 a turn the engine daemon drives starts and reads them in the daemon (`crates/nekko-tools` `process`), where they also die with the daemon, a crash included (its job object on Windows); a chat that uses tool hooks runs them in the daemon too since its hooks moved there.
- **Steer a running reply, and questions that do not wait for ever** `[shipped 2026-10-04]`, the two things a person needs from an agent they are not sitting next to. **Steering**: while a reply runs, **Ctrl/⌘+Enter** in the composer (or **Steer** on a queued follow-up) sends the text into the running turn instead of queuing it or stopping the reply: it joins the transcript as a user message at the next tool boundary, never mid-stream, so the next model call reads it and the work so far is kept (pi's steering queue, typing into a working Claude Code or Codex session). Enter still queues for after the reply; a reply with no tool step left (it was already writing its answer) queues the steering text to run next. Both loops take it (`pullSteering` in the TS loop, `loop:steer` into the daemon run's inbox). **Unattended questions**: Settings → Agent loop → *Unattended questions: decide after N minutes* (default 0, wait for ever) puts a limit on how long an `ask_user` question waits; past it the agent is told nobody answered and to choose the most reasonable, least destructive option itself and state the assumption, so four chats left running overnight do not each park on a question. Approvals of risky commands never time out this way.
